## Memnon

Memnon was a key port of trade and garrison city for the kingdom of Calimshan. In the distant past, it began life as part of the city of Memnonnar, capital of the efreet kingdom of Memnonnar. Following the Spellplague and the return of its original founder and namesake, the efreeti Memnon, it became the capital of a reborn Memnonnar, ruled by genies and genasi. Eventually, in the late 15 century DR, the enslaved populace of Calimshan rose up and overthrew their tyrannical rulers, returning human rule to Memnon.

Geography
Memnon was situated on the northern border of the Calim Desert, at its very edges, where it met the Trackless Sea. It stood at the mouth of the River Agis, otherwise known as the River Memnon, and along the Trade Way. It was also connected to Calimport by way of the Coast Trail, which skirted along the Calim Desert's edge.

Description
Allegedly smaller in size than the capital city Calimport, it was no less an impressively large metropolis, rivaling some of the great cities of the Sword Coast. Its streets were vast and labyrinthine. Much of the city's architecture was made up of stone, clay, and mudbrick. Like nearly every settlement in Calimshan, Memnon was a walled and fortified city. Its outer walls and the large block tower that overlooked its main northeastern gate, dating back to the Era of Skyfire, were composed of a smooth dark rock. This rock had the unique properties of being totally immune to wear and heat, withstanding even the likes of fireballs and fiery dragon breath. No matter the local conditions, the shale-like stone was always cool to the touch and shined. But when removed from the walls proper, the stone miraculously became mere coal.

The most striking feature of Memnon's architecture was its scarlet brickwork—the local rich red clay and mud brightened into this shade as they were baked—which could be denoted from great distances. Some of the upperclasses of Memnon viewed this scarlet shade as the "commoners' red" and paid for stone and tiles, imported from elsewhere in Calimshan and the far off Sword Coast, or even salvaged from the ruins of Memnonnar, to construct homes and businesses with.

Many of the general housing structures were squat, clay buildings of a rectangular shape, measuring a dozen feet across. They faced out towards the Trackless Sea, allowing the sea breeze to waft onto them, and were equipped with awnings. Towering over them were mighty palaces made of marble and tall, thin towers domed with golf leaf. In the poorer southwestern district of the city, many houses had flimsy walls and floors made of stone and dirt. Even poorer housing were shacks made from driftwood. The city sprawl spread out into the docks and lining the docks were a number of windowless warehouses.

On a hill overlooking the southwestern-most point of Memnon's harbor, behind a rocky outcropping, a patch of dirt was used as  burial ground amongst the destitute. The city proper had its own cemetery, full of commoner markhouts and filigreed khamarkhas.

In the years spent under genasi rule following the Spellplague, the city came to be transformed into an even more magnificent metropolis, full of glittering palaces and verdant villas.

Beneath the depths of Memnon, one could find ancient and oft sealed tunnels connecting to Old Shanatar.
Organization
While ignorant foreigners visiting Memnon sometimes felt that the city seemed as though it was laid out without any kind of civic planning, in truth Memnon was built using the "drudach system", much like nearly every other settlement throughout Calimshan. This Calishite form of urban structuring, established all the way back to when it was the rule of genies, consisted of an interlocking series of small brick-walled neighborhoods called drudachs. All drudachs were further grouped together into subdivisons known as sabbans, whose boundaries were similarly marked by low mud-brick walls. Each sabban worked as a self-sufficient town—it had its own water supplies, guards, markets, residences, and so on. The city had a total of seven sabbans and twenty-six drudachs.

Like other port cities in Calimshan, Memnon had piers and wharves that were specially designated for use by the navy. Known as the Naval Sabban, it also enclosed the shipwrights and naval barracks.

Government
The rulers of Memnon were referred to as "sultans", a title equivalent to "prince" amongst the Calishites. During the period of genasi rule, the title changed to that of Vizier. The later return of human rule to the city came with a resurgence of the "sultan" title.

The official ruler of Memnon in 1370 DR was Sultan Arin yn Kahar el Sujil, who had been installed by his father the Syl-Pasha Ralan el Pesarkhal through political and trade alliances. However, the real power was held by his vizar, Anders el Gnurlbrach. Despite being a half-orc, since at least 1357 DR, Anders was very popular both with the general populace of Memnon and with the syl-pasha of Calimshan. This was in part because he established an elected city council that he voluntarily shared power with. He encouraged the other pashas and vizars beneath him to leave Arin in place, for purposes of maintaining a good appearance. The two of them were spied upon on behalf of the syl-pasha by Arin's cousins, the lesser vizars Asraf and Kadar, a fact that Anders knew all too well.

Almost all magic and wizardry in the city were controlled and regulated by the Guild Arcane. Memnon long had a reputation for being friendly towards wizards, but fawned over magic far less than other Calishite cities.
Law & Order
In 1370 DR, around 750 amlakkar were stationed in the city, who acted as the city watch. Memnon's amlakkar were considered to be some of the most heavily armed in all of Calimshan, though their armaments and armor paled in comparison to those of the soldiers garrisoned within the Defender's Drudach. The duties of the amlakkar notably included defending the Theater of All Faiths.

The city's harbor was overseen by the Memnon Harbor Patrol, who plied around the busy waters in longboats with a beacon lantern at their bow a discerning flag at their stern. They searched all incoming vessels for contraband. Those found to be carrying contraband were to be denied access to the port, though in practice patrolmen tried to wring out bribes to be allowed access into the port. They also oversaw the detention of pirates, offering a reward for any captured and brought into port.
Crime
In 1370 DR, the vast majority of criminal enterprises in Memnon were controlled by El Pesarkhal, one of the nation's four major crime families. They were led by Nasim yn Feroz, the self-proclaimed "Dark Caleph" and brother to Syl-Pasha Ralan. Serving as the lieutenant of Memnon was Basad yn Malik. Nasim was a loyal subject to the syl-pasha, the underworld agencies being inter-meshed with the government system of Calimshan.

El Pesarkhal primarily presided over blackmarketeering, thuggery, enforcement, and spying. They also had direct control over the other crime families, such as the piracy and smuggling focused El Vyndahla of Almraiven, who had major links to enterprises in Memnon. The other prominent crime family in Memnon were El Khamir. They oversaw the bulk of the piracy and smuggling that went on in Memnon, as well as the slave trade, burglars, thieves, and assassins. Additionally, the greater assassination trade across Calimshan was centered in Memnon.

According to some, Memnon also boasted one of the best organized beggar's guild in Faerûn, with very strict rates. They'd charge for any service, even helping someone up if they'd fallen, and just to spit-polish a sword was 10 copper pieces.The term "guild" is used euphemistically in Calimshan to refer to illegal trades that were operated and controlled by the major crime families of Calimshan (see Empires of the Shining Sea, p.71) With that in mind, it's possible that this "beggar's guild" is actually part of El Khamir, as they oversaw thief activity. Foreigners were often targeted by the beggars, as they typically brought wealth to Memnon. By the late 15 century DR, this beggar's guild was still around.

Other criminal organizations with a presence in Memnon included the Rundeen, the Many Long Arms, the Twisted Rune, and the Shadow Thieves, who controlled a school for assassins within the city known as the School of Stealth. The Twisted Rune were represented by the dracolich Sapphiraktar, who in the 14 century DR was the puppet master of over a third of important underworld and government figures in the city, all being merely illusory disguises or projections. One of these disguises, a corrupt baelnorn who others believed was restricted to the subterranean depths of Memnon, had a monopoly over the corpse trade and trade of necromantic supplies throughout Calimshan.

The Janessar, an outlaw anti-slavery organization, also had scattered hideouts in Memnon.

During the period of genasi rule, the Twisted Rune were able to forge alliances with genasi leaders in Memnon. This period also saw the active presence of remnants of the Janessar, helping Memnonite slaves to escape to the human-controlled city of Almraiven.

Trade
Memnon was a key trade city for Calimshan. It was that kingdom's northernmost port, the first port for goods coming down from the north and the last Calishite port for goods heading northward. On some days over a hundred ships, flying the flags of a hundred different ports of a dozen different kingdoms, could be found anchored outside a full port.

Nearly all caravaneers on the Trade Way stopped at Memnon for at least a day, regardless of whether they were traveling north or south. The city thus served the function of a massive caravanserai, offering various caravan services. These included offering inns, stables, wagon building and repairs, supplies and equipment, or entertainment to those who'd just survived the trek across the desert or to those about to make it.

Altogether the festhalls, inns, and taverns of Memnon numbered around 600 or so. These establishment all typically set their prices according to supply and demand, leaving them to be higher than the going rates elsewhere in Calimshan, even for the crudest of accommodations. The best a traveler on a tight budget could hope for was a hayloft bed and a daily handmeal. Additionally, like many cities in the nation, Memnon's tavern landscape was dominated by Golden Sands affiliated taverns. These were denoted by having either "Golden" or "Sands" in their name.

Additionally, Memnon was considered a fishing town, producing both a high quantity and a high quality of fish, shrimp, and shellfish of many varieties. Much of the city's money was tied to its fishing industry. Other major products of Memnon included mercenaries, shipwrights, and pottery. Some of the prosperous pashas of Memnon were known to call upon mercenaries to collect debts or settle scores. All manner of other goods could be found in its markets, including exotic pipeweeds, perfume, slaves, and oil lamps.

During the Tethyrian Interregnum, the city played a key role in the Calishite slave trade, acting as a staging point for slaves that had been captured from the lawless land of Tethyr. But after Zaranda Star was crowned Queen of Tethyr in 1369 DR and restored law to the land, the city became less appealing Calishite slavers, with talks of relocating to either Athkatla or Murann. Following the Spellplague, slavery became one of Memnon's keystone industries, with those that could be spared from the war effort being forced to mine a special dark rock in the nearby Marching Mountains, the same type of dark rock that the walls around the city were made of by during the First Era of Skyfire. The lives of these slave miners were usually brutal and short, further complicated by raids from orcs and ogres that called the Marching Mountains home.

Once every year, the city sent a heavily armed contingent of guards and stonecutters across the River Agis into the ruins of Memnonnar. There they scavenged stone for use in building repairs as well as decorative usages. By 1370 DR, this tradition had been going on for centuries.

By the late 15 century DR, some of the Chultan merchant princes of Port Nyanzaru had established outposts in Memnon.

Defenses
The city had a strong military focus. It served as the northernmost naval base for the Qysanallojal, Calimshan's navy, as well as their second primary port. Their warships and soldiers were considered essential to combating northern aggression and any monsters and other horrors that could emerge from the ruins of Memnonnar across the River Agis. Many wizards also served within the navy, acted as navigational aids, or provided other unconventional defenses against the pirates of the Trackless Sea.

In 1370 DR, over 2000 to 3000 soldiers were garrisoned in Memnon within the Defender's Drudach. All were outfitted with bristling armor and gleaming weaponry.

The ships docked in Memnon were the smallest and fastest in the Qysanallojal, designed for scouting and anti-piracy purposes. In 1357 DR, the navy was growing so quickly that fishermen were being forced to find new docks. On average there were no fewer than 30 to 35 ships moored in Memnon at any given time. In addition to their assigned crews, these ships were tended to on and off board in 1370 DR by roughly 700 people.

Following the Spellplague, the city's heavy militaristic focus was continued by its new leaders, who fed part of the enslaved populace into their ongoing war with Calimport. Their constant fighting across the Calim Desert became a source of infamy among the rest of Faerûn. Even many of the Memnonite citizenry disapproved of the city's warmongering.

Culture
Events & Festivals
In the wake of the loss of their genie leaders in 1450 DR, factions from the cities of Memnon, Calimport, and Almraiven came together to organize a race across the Calim Desert,The adventure refers to this race tradition as having been going on "for the past 30 years." Since the date of the adventure is assumed to be 1479 DR, it more likely began in the year when the Second Era of Skyfire came to an end, 1450 DR. starting at an oasis somewhere between Calimport and Memnon. To the champion of this race would be afforded a large gem referred to as the "Heart of the Desert". This race went on to become a triennial event among the three cities, each time with the ultimate prize being the largest gem found in the prior three years.
Religion
All of the major churches of the Faerûnian pantheon were represented among the clergical population of Memnon, with nearly all having some sort of temple. Some of the many deities worshiped in Memnon included Ibrandul, Selune, Shaundakul, Silvanus, Talona, Umberlee, and Valkur.

Very few of the temples in Memnon were major complexes, like those found in Calimport and Keltar. And despite being a port city, Memnon did not boast any more temples to gods of water than inland cities. One of the most prominent religious structures in the city was the Theater of All Faiths. Once a theater and stadium under the days of genie ruler, it became a venue for religious ceremonies by any and all faiths.

Inhabitants
The bulk of Memnon's populace was made up of humans. Beyond them there were prominent numbers of half-orcs, halflings, and elves living in the city. The overall population was sizeable, numbering around the thirty thousands, though foreign visitors had a tendency to over-estimate the number of people living in the city.In the novel The Stowaway, protagonist Maimun muses that the population of Memnon was likely greater than that of Baldur's Gate. Similarly, The Halfling's Gem has the narrator musing that Memnon's population was comparable to "...all the people of the cities along the northern stretches of the Sword Coast, Waterdeep included..." Consulting books set close to the time of the novel, Forgotten Realms Adventures gives Baldur's Gate a population size of 86,000+, whilst Waterdeep and the North gives the eponymous city a population of around 122,000+. At the time of writing The Halfling's Gem (1990), it's possible that author R.A. Salvatore was basing his estimation off of the population size of 310,000 given for Memnon in Empires of the Sands (1988), but this number was later retconned in Empires of the Shining Sea (1998) as having been over-inflated in-universe by Calimshan's census-takers.

The central and eastern quarters of the city were occupied by caravan travelers and the local nobility, with the city's sailors rarely venturing far beyond the Naval Sabban and the drudachs that surrounded it.

In the 14 century DR, homeless was an ongoing issue in the city, with many in Memnon's poorer southwestern district having little choice but to sleep on the sandy streets. Many of the poorest of the destitute suffered maladies of malnourishment and poor hygiene.

Following the Spellplague, efreet and firesoul genasi made up a prominent portion of the population and became the upper class of society, enslaving the rest of the population. Like their counterparts in Calimport, the genasi were notorious for their treatment of humans as slaves. Among the genasi, the firesouls pledged to the efreet were believed to be superior to all other elemental souls, but the earthsouled were considered the lowest ranking amongst all of the aspects of the genasi.

There were also large numbers of migrant tieflings in the city, many of whom were put to work in Memnon's military, earning positions of power and respect among the fire genasi elite.

History
Memnonnar
In, the Great Pasha Memnon and his efreet Army of Fire arrived in southern Faerûn through a portal known as the Great Brass Gate. Near where they had arrived on the Prime Material, close to the River Agis, he founded the river-spanning city of Memnonnar and it would become the capital to a kingdom by the same name. His armies forced the local dwarves of Deep Shanatar to build this city and clear down the forests for where it would stand.

Circa, a centuries long conflict between Memnon and the djinni Calim, known as the First Era of Skyfire, came to a close. Elven high mages from the communities of Darthiir Wood, Tethir, and Shilmista came together to enact a plan of ending their terror. They magically infiltrated the city of Memnon, as well as the city of Schamedar and the ruins of Teshyll. Once inside these sites, they used their elven high magic to create a powerful spell that would be unleashed at the Battle of Teshyllal Fields by the High Mage Pharos. Memnon and his rival Calim would both be locked away, their life forces and those of over a hundred of their genie followers all fused together into what became known as the Calimemnon Crystal.

With the loss of its leader, it wasn't long before the remaining inhabitants of Memnonnar fell prey to the elves of Darthiir Wood. Over the next 30 years, all traces of the city of Memnonnar were ruins. What survived of it on the southern side of the River Agis would one day go on to become the site of the city of Memnon.

High Shanatar
In the, seeing the need to prevent the expansion of giants in the Marching Mountains, King Adiir Velm led 5,000 dwarves out of Deep Shanatar and up to the plains of old Memnonnar. Sometime over the next four to six centuries, the dwarves occupied the southern ruins of Memnonnar and built among them their own great city as part of High Shanatar.
Calishite Empire
In the, needing to transport troops to their frontier wars against the elves of Tethir, the the Calimshan Empire used slave labor to build a road between the cities of Memnon and Zazesspur. Almost immediately, this military road became dominated by merchant traffic.

In the, following the Throne Wars of Calimshan, the Syl-Pasha Tasyn el Tarshaj yi Manshaka and his family simultaneously conquered the cities of Memnon, Almraiven, Calimport, and Manshaka, unifying the Pasha's Lands.

In the, Memnon is hit by the Shaking Plague.

In the, Akkabar Shoon rose to power as the Syl-Vizar and ruler of Memnon. Over the next seven months, through careful political maneuvering, Akkabar would go on to become the Syl-Pasha of all of Calimshan, founding the Shoon Imperium.
Shoon Imperium
Over the course of the Shoon Imperium, the Guilds Arcane were established by the ruling Qysars. The last of these to be established would be in Memnon.

In the, with Umberlant worship waning among Calishites after more than a century of peaceful waters, five massive waves struck at different points across the Shoon Imperium. The cities of Almraiven, Manshaka, Teshburl, Memnon, and Calimport. About a third to two-thirds of each settlement was severely damaged by flooding. Through this terror Umberlee inspired renewed reverence among the Calishites. Since that day, Memnon was never without at least two structures dedicated to Umberlee, for fear that lack of reverence would bring the "Vengeance Waves" to bear upon them once more.

In 75 DR, cities across the Shoon Imperium are hit by Fog Fever. Memnon, Calimport, and Myratma are hit hardest by the plague.

In 138 DR, Memnon is among a number of cities in the Shoon Imperium to suffer great fires within its poorer, wooden sections. While likely the result of fireworks the Qysar had demanded for his birthday, Qsar Amahl Shoon IV blamed the incidinces on the Church of Ilmater.

In 319 DR, with rates of crime and violence increasing across the Imperium, Qysar Shoon VII decided to take drastic measures. He ordered oil to be poured into the sewers of major imperial cities, causing the Great Fires. While originally planned to be controlled blazes, the fires raged almost unchecked. With a death toll reaching over three million people, the cities of Memnon, Ankhapur, Calimport, Innarlith, and Ithmong were near completely devastated.

In 320 DR, Memnon is among the cities of Calimshan to get hit by the Infernal Death.

Calimshan
In 1334 DR, during the tumultous Ninth Age of Calimshan, rumors circulated in Memnon that the upstart kingdom of Mulsparkh was eyeing the city as a new base of operations. Before long troops from Memnon were sent out to aid the army of Tethyr in the Battle of Nightflames, bringing an end to Mulsparkh.

In the late 1340s DR, Conner and Vaji ran confidence tricks in all the major cities of Calimshan, including Memnon, before they went on to Manshaka.

In the, Vajra, Conner, and Salabak sailed back into Memnon on the trail of the mage Chancil Crystalheart. However, his thugs and apprentices waylaid them with a wand of telekinesis, hurling them high into the air. Fortunately their fall was broken by an orange-seller's stall, and they were surrounded by eager-to-help beggars, which Salabak drove off with the illusion of a squealer. They followed Chancil's apprentice to his home, where they attempted to rescue the kidnapped cat child.

In the, in the month of Hammer, a great spell battle broke out in the streets of Memnon between the archmages Galthaunt and Yuthrygg and their many magical creations, after the latter had declared himself "Overking of Tethyr". Spanning most of a day, Memnonites fled the city in terror.

Under the rule of Syl-Pasha Ralan el Pesarkhal, both the Guilds Arcane of Calimport and Memnon were relied upon in ongoing efforts to restore safety to the Trade Road.

Post-Spellplague
After the Spellplague of the, the High Magic that bound Calim and Memnon in the Calimemnon Crystal shattered, unleashing them upon the world. Freed, the two genies resumed their millennia-old hostilities, overthrowing the Calimshan government and ushering in the Second Era of Skyfire or "Memnon's Blessed Return" as some referred to it.

In the wake of the return of Calim and Memnon, many of the residents of Calimshan, presumed to be human, manifested their genasi heritage. These descendants of the elemental powers pledged themselves to either elemental fire or air. Those who became firesoul genasi took over the city of Memnon, joining its namesake in his war against the djinn and windsoul genasi who ceased control of Calimport. With this, the old realm of Calimshan effectively ceased to exist, with the firesoul genasi followers of Memnon proclaiming the whole region to be Memnonnar reborn.

The Second Era of Skyfire lasted until 1450 DR, when both Memnon and Calim were banished to the Elemental Chaos by an unknown event. They left in their wake a devastated Calimshan, torn apart by the remaining genasi warlords. This period also saw an The influx of exotic and valuable goods to Memnon, making the city a tempting target for overly ambitious thieves and adventurers to plunder.

By 1489 DR, the human slaves of Calimshan had overthrown their genasi masters with the aid of a Chosen of Ilmater. Though the slaves initially used nonviolent methods in resisting the genasi, their efforts escalated into full rebellion after the Chosen disappeared. These rebellions deposed the genie lords of Calimport and Memnon, sending them either to the Elemental Planes or the deep deserts. Free of their genie masters, the people of Calimshan needed to find new ways to live together and tolerate those genasi who remained. Many Calishites prayed for the Chosen to come back and complete his work.

Notable Locations
 The Theater of All Faiths: An ancient theater later used as a temple.
 The Golden Grove: A druid's grove dedicated to Silvanus.
 The Protectors' House: A temple that was jointly shared by the faiths of Selûne, Shaundakul, Valkur, and Ibrandul. Formerly, it was all a temple to Bhaal.
 The Fine Gold Chain: An adventurers' club that looked like a castle out of a fairy-tale.
 The Brazen Behir: A popular inn.
 The Black Flagon: A classic tavern.
 Plaza of Divine Truth
 Sacred Hunter's Lodge
 Tulbusker's Safe Haven: A cozily shabby inn operated by a former adventurer. It was a favored venue among many visitors to Memnon.
 Khanduq of the Coinmother: A large khanduq in the city.

Notable Inhabitants
 Aborana Startoucher, a famous diviner, was a former resident.
 Artemis Entreri, the infamous assassin, was born here.
 Chancil Crystalheart, a notable criminal mage who lived in a palatial home in Memnon.
 Drakosa, a petty noble who in 1370 DR was the sole Calishite landholder of the New Amnian colonies.
 Fual al Barasth, a low-ranking druzir of a military drudach in 1370 DR.
 Rhaunaguth, a mage who lived as a nazir in Memnon during the Shoon Imperium, overseeing the city's outlying plantations.

## Mezro

Before its "destruction" in the Spellplague, Mezro was the largest civilized population center in Chult. It was a holy place to the Tabaxi people, being founded by their god Ubtao.

Geography and Layout
Mezro was built along the River Olung, which served as its southern and western borders. Thick jungle was found on the east and north.

The city was circular and divided into four quarters by wide avenues stemming from the Temple of Ubtao at the center.

Agricultural Quarter
This quarter was located in the southeast. It was divided up into farms, fields, and groves for growing bananas, berries, cassavas, nuts, papayas, plantains, sorghum, yams, and various medicinal plants. Honey was also produced here. The land was magically enhanced to ensure fertile crops. Small dinosaurs were common in this area of the city.

Residential Quarter
This quarter was located in the southwest. It was a literal maze of simple, one-story, adobe-style houses. Most Mezroans lived here. The byways were of sand with rushes.

Market Quarter
This quarter was located in the northwest. Here were found craftsmen, hostels, inns, and various smiths. Also located here was the famous amphitheater built by Ubtao himself.

Scholars' Quarter
This quarter was located in the northeast. It contained the Library of Mezro, the College of Wizards, and the Warriors' Training Ground. It was full of other schools as well, as the city educated every child in math, history, and literacy, and guilds were not present there to teach crafts.

Notable Locations

 Amphitheater of Mezro  The center of Mezroan cultural life, this stadium was decorated with gem-studded dinosaur statues and was the site of recitals of Chultan lore, plays, and dinosaur races.
 College of Wizards  This active school of magic was highly guarded to non-students.
 Library of Mezro  This three-story-tall building housed parchment books detailing over fifteen hundred years of Mezroan culture. It also included a museum of statues, paintings, and musical instruments.
 Temple of Ubtao  Created by Ubtao, this ancient, magical, nine-story, golden-domed temple was found at the center of Mezro. It contains a Hall of Champions and the King's audience hall, from which the bara Osaw ruled.

Government
Mezro was a theocracy ruled by the barae, the Chosen of Ubtao. Each bara lived forever and was granted unique powers by Ubtao. There were seven in number.

At the end of the 14 century DR, the barae were:
 Osaw I
 Kwalu
 Dhalmass Rayburton
 Alisanda Rayburton
 Mainu
 Fipya
 Ras Nsi (exiled)

Tabaxi warriors patrolled the city of Mezro and would brand any lawbreakers with a blue triangle on their forehead. Punishment for murder might be execution, but most crimes resulted in just compensation.

History
The god Ubtao founded the city of Mezro in . He raised its temple and amphitheater directly from the chaos of the jungle.

In the Year of Blooded Sunsets (−137 DR), during the great war between the Tabaxi and the Eshowe, the shadow creature Eshowdow nearly brought the city to ruin.

The entire city became invisible around 863 DR. This was caused by a magical wall that was built around it. Not only did the wall render the city invisible but it also caused magical confusion to whomever came too close to its walls. It remained hidden like this for nearly 500 years until 1363 DR, when the rulers of Mezro decided to lower the magical defenses after a solid victory against the Batiri goblin tribe. After that time, travelers were once again welcomed in the city, and it saw a surge in growth.

In The Year of Blue Flame, 1385 DR, when the Spellplague struck the world, a great cataclysm separated the Chultan Peninsula from the rest of the continent. To save the city from destruction, as well as from Ras Nsi ambitions, the barae created a demiplane and transported their city and all its citizens there. They left behind artificial ruins to discourage Ras Nsi and every other troublemaker from finding what really happened to the city. According to the bara Alisanda and the naga Saja N'baza, Mezro would not return to Toril as for as long Ras Nsi was alive.

For the rest of the world, Mezro was destroyed during the Spellplague. By 1479 DR, the artificial ruins of Mezro were an undead- and monster-infested site. At some point during the late years of 1480s DR, members of the Flaming Fist looted most of the ruins and began to use them as training grounds for their forces in Chult.

## Melkar

Melkar of Mirabar was a mage who tried to breach the protections around Ahghairon's Tower in 1271 DR (although his identity was not public knowledge). He failed, having misjudged the number of keys needed by half. His skeleton remained hanging inside the protections as a warning.

In 1479 DR, Melkar's journal was retrieved by Samark "the Blackstaff" Dhanzscul from the tomb of Khelben Arunsun. It fell into the hands of Samark's killers, Khondar and Centiv Naomal, who used it to determine the number of keys needed to enter the tower.

## Melegaunt Tanthul

Prince Melegaunt Tanthul was one of the youngest Princes of Shade.

Personality
He had a knack for deception, combined with a talent of false empathy, that made him a formidable manipulator. There were few people outside the royal family that he could not win over with his combination of charm, good looks, and seeming sincerity. Indeed, he practically made it his life's work. It was believed that he tricked Galaeron Nihmedu into opening the Sharn Wall in Evereska, thus unleashing an army of Phaerimm on Faerûn.

Relationships
Melegaunt had twelve brothers, but among them, only Dethud Tanthul favored his policy of manipulation.

Abilities
Melegaunt was a spy from Thultanthar, also referred to as the City of Shade. He had a barely above average aptitude in the magical arts, but was trained exactly like the rest of the princes and the secret kept safe on pain of death by order of his father, Telamont. What Melegaunt lacked in arcane aptitude, however, he made up for in guile.

History
 Early Life
Like the rest of his brothers, Melegaunt was born before Karsus's Folly in Netheril at some point. In accordance with the rules of Thultanthar, Melegaunt underwent tests at the age of 13 to determine his aptitude. However the Determinists found that Melegaunts potential with magic was only just above average. Despite this his father, Telamont Tanthul, decreed that Melegaunt would still be trained in the arcane arts anyway and that the results of the test kept secret.

Traveling Toril
Melegaunt arrived on Toril in 1222 DR and began exploring and studying the world. Over the next few decades he made his way from Kara-Tur to Chult and even Halruaa to Hartsvale.

In 1269 DR, while traveling through Vaasa, Melegaunt rescued Bodvar and the rest of the Moor Eagle clan. Afterwards the Prince of Shade managed to convince Bodvar to let him stay with the Vaasans. During his time among the Moor Eagles, Melegaunt constructed 20 darkswords for the clan's warriors but Bodvar initially refused the weapons. Eventually Bodvar and his clan were forced to take up the blades against a horde of monstrous humanoids, binding themselves in service to the shadovar wizard.

 Spying on the Phaerimm
By 1271 DR, Melegaunt had left the Moor Eagles after constructing the Granite Tower and entered the Anauroch on his own. There he began mapping the Buried Realms of the Underdark and spying on the phaerimm. Despite his skill with deception, in 1346 DR the phaerimm eventually became aware of his presence which brought Melegaunt into frequent conflict with the aberrations.

 Return of the Archwizards
By 1371 DR, Melegaunt had gathered enough information on the phaerimm. He called upon Bodvar's pact and gathered 20 skilled Vaasan warriors, led by Vala Thorsdotter, to assist him in the crypts beneath the Shaeradim. However on Nightal 20, while exploring the crypts, Melagaunt and his warriors crossed a band of Evereskan tomb guardians led by Galaeron Nihmedu. During the ensuing conflict, Melegaunt's Shadow Weave magic and Galaeron's Weave magic collided and the resulting interaction created a breach in the Sharn Wall, which allowed the phaerimm to escape into Faerûn.

Despite this calamity, Melegaunt slowly worked his way into Galaeron's confidence and even unveiled the secrets of the Shadow Weave to the elf. Vala and Melegaunt traveled with Galaeron to the High Forest in search of the Karsestone which Melegaunt explained could defeat the phaerimm.

On Hammer 1 of 1372 DR Melegaunt died in battle against the lich Wulgreth of Ascalhorn within a pyramid in Karse Butte. Although Melegaunt perished, he had managed to subvert Galaeron enough that the tomb guardian completed the ritual to summon the City of Shade in Melegaunt's stead.

## Mentalism domain

The Mentalism domain was a deity domain that granted divine spellcasters like clerics with spells and powers based around mentalism.

Deities

Powers
A priest with this domain could use abjuration magic to temporarily ward their mind or the mind of another against external effects, giving them the will to resist. It lasted one hour and could be done once a day.

Spells
As they advanced, a priest with this domain could cause a foe to act randomly with lesser confusion, to read minds with detect thoughts, to see or hear from afar with clairaudience/clairvoyance, to modify memory, to distract others with a mind fog, to communicate with allies via a Rary's telepathic bond, to repel creatures with antipathy, to shield a mind with mind blank, and finally to voyage to the Astral Plane using astral projection.

## Mercenary

A mercenary was an individual skilled in fighting, usually a soldier, who was paid to fight on behalf of an individual, group, country, organization, or other military force. Mercenaries were located all throughout Faerûn.

Services

People who had the funds to hire mercenaries usually did it because they were already using their most trusted and able warriors as generals or personal bodyguards, so they needed professional warriors to serve as the bulk of their fighting force. They usually hired mercenaries to pillage, raid, and other similar activities normal armies usually couldn't or wouldn't do.

Mercenaries were also hired by merchants as bodyguards, cargo loaders and unloaders, and guards for warehouses, shops, cargo, and wagons.

Prices

Standard mercenary rates were around 1 gp per day per soldier, plus three meals, tents, and boots, and a bonus (usually 5 gp) for every major battle won. Employers could expect to pay as much as 25 gp per day per soldier. Mercenary leaders were also paid a large negotiated amount for expenses upon hiring, a 1,000 gp bonus for the achievement of agreed-upon objectives, and a large negotiated fulfillment fee when the ultimate objective of their campaign was achieved.

Such payments were made to surviving mercenaries and never to the kin of the fallen, unless a fellow mercenary wanted to help the surviving relatives of a comrade-in-arms. This was different in some regions, such as in Chessenta and east and south of Raurin, as mercenaries from there had a ransom price that their families or treasuries could pay if they were captured. Heralds and gods frowned upon and publicly denounced those who collected a ransom and then delivered a mistreated, near-death captive, or freed the victim far from home in dangerous territory so that he or she could well be recaptured and ransomed again.

Heralds and priests of Tempus always witnessed the signing of and retained copies of agreements between a mercenary and their patrons, to make both parties uphold their part of the agreements. If employers didn't fulfill their agreement, or if they failed or purposefully didn't pay the mercenaries, the heralds and priests would publicly announce their treachery, staining the reputation of such patrons forever (meaning no mercenary company would ever work for them again), and even Tempus himself would frown on their battle fortune.

Mercenaries that betrayed their oaths and agreements were known as "dullblades" (as also were inexperienced mercenaries). Dullblades were considered expendable and if hired, they were usually assigned the most dangerous works, with a bottom-rate payment: 1 cp a day plus two daily meals, a bed blanket, and a wound-dressing (a wash and bandages).

Mercenaries working for merchants had cheaper rates, as merchants usually paid 3 sp per day, plus two meals, a secure and private room with a bed, and a period of vacation, usually of two days every tenday. This payment was a common fee among many merchants.

Background

Mercenaries had an everlasting reputation for unreliability, because they were fighting for gold instead of for a cause, and many believed mercenaries would put their own needs—such as saving their own lives in a battle—over those of their patrons. For those reasons, some people preferred to hire professional adventurers over mercenaries.

However, most of the people who needed a fighting force liked to hire mercenaries for those same reasons. Mercenaries had to be neutral in conflicts or, as said by older mercenaries, be "bloodsworn" to one side, and thus could not dare switch sides during a conflict for fear of damaging their reputation as trustworthy mercenaries. Mercenaries also dared not switch sides because Heralds and priests of Tempus would proclaim their deeds to everyone, so no one would hire them thereafter.

History
Around the 1350s DR onward, most large mercenary companies began to dwindle and disappear. By the mid-1360s DR, only a handful of companies remained. This situation didn't changed in the 15 century DR, with large mercenary companies being uncommon by the late 1480s DR.

Notable Mercenaries
Oadro Skorlapras, an Amnian mercenary leader who met his end in Tethyr after the restoration of Tethyr's monarchy in 1369 DR.

Trivia
 In Common, mercenaries that demonstrably lacked principles or loyalty to the patrons that hired them were often referred to by the slang term "Darkblade".

## Menzoberranzan (game)

Menzoberranzan is a computer game developed by DreamForge Intertainment and published by Strategic Simulations in 1994. It is based on the Advanced Dungeons and Dragons 2nd edition system and is the first video game to star Drizzt Do'Urden.

Gameplay
Menzoberranzan is a first-person role-playing game, similar in style to Eye of the Beholder, Ultima Underworld, and Might and Magic. The game uses the same engine that was used for Ravenloft: Strahd's Possession.

The player starts with two heroes in a small village, which is under attack by evil drow. They follow them into the Underdark to the mighty drow city of Menzoberranzan. Along the way, they encounter companions of varying races, and fight off foes from gnolls to yochlols.

Plot

Prologue
A group of local adventurers from a village in Icewind Dale set out to slay a monstrous beast. During the battle, a member of the party thrusts a spear into its neck. Despite injuring the creature, the spear-fighter is torn from the ground, and eaten alive. As the battle continues, the party's magic-user casts a mighty spell at the many-legged creature. The foe is slain, and the two remaining heroes return to the village to the greetings and cheers of the locals. But as the festivities carry on late into the night, the village is raided by a drow party. The drow invasion force capture a group of villagers and escape with them, and the heroes are charged with a quest to retrieve them.

First Steps
As the heroes take their first steps out of the village tavern to aid in the defense against the remaining drow, they also have to put out a fire started in the village store. When the invaders have been repelled, and the fire put out, the heroes are given a special helmet by the village's innkeeper as a reward.

The two heroes venture out into the plains of Icewind Dale, and they encounter gnolls, drow, verbeeg and other monsters. As they move around the plains, they find the house of the wise old druid Vermulean, who knows a lot about the world and its wonders. He tells the heroes that to venture into the Underdark, they will need protective stones so that the radiation doesn't harm them and to allow them to see as an underground dweller would. This leads the heroes to quest for the gems all over the plains. Their hunt leads them to meet Drizzt Do'Urden in a mountain pass, where he saves them from fighting a giant and offers to join up with the heroes.

Further away in the plains, the heroes meet the kenku Vonar, a friend to Drizzt, who had his wing wounded by the drow who invaded the village. He also offers to join the party. After gathering all the gems, they take them to the old wise man who transforms the gems, so that the heroes may venture into the Underdark.

Into the Underdark
See Helm of Spiders for the full plot involving the drow artifact.

Although they do not know it, the gem and helmet they received are actually parts of the legendary Helm of Spiders, an ancient and powerful drow artifact. The party ventures deeper into the Underdark, finding friends and foes along the way, some of which join the adventurers. They travel through dwarven mines, the Temple of Dumathoin, and caverns of myconids, galeb duhr and driders. On their journey, they find out much about the treachery of the dark elves, and eventually reach their infamous city of Menzoberranzan.

Here, they find themselves amid raging wars between drow houses, all vying for the ancient Helm of Spiders. After the heroes recover the last part of the ancient artifact, the adventurers use it to rescue the prisoners. Drizzt leads them out of the Underdark, and all the villagers are united again.

Trivia
 The box's front cover features a drawing of Drizzt Do'Urden by Jeff Easley, which was also used as the front cover to R.A. Salvatore's novel, Homeland. Likewise, the back of the box and hint-book cover feature an image of Menzoberranzan, both of which were also used for the second book in the Menzoberranzan box set.
 As well as an automapping feature, the game had a printing feature. If the user clicked the printer icon, their local unit would print out the same map their party was exploring, which the player could then annotate by hand.

## Merrow (ogre)

Merrow, also known as sea ogres or aquatic ogres, were a breed of ogre adapted to aquatic environments.

Description
Merrow living in freshwater environs were more violent and quicker than land ogres. They had green, scaled skin, green eyes, and seaweed-green, slimy hair. They had black teeth and nails; webbed hands and feet; long, thick necks; underbites; and sloping shoulders. One in ten merrow had ivory horns, especially the most powerful males.

Merrow enjoyed tattooing their bodies. Females, especially, were often found covered in depictions of death and destruction all over their bodies.

Personality
Merrow were ignorant and superstitious brutes; their only skills were plundering and murder.

Merrow enjoyed gold and jewels and often overlooked dull magical items in favor of glittering prizes.

Biology
Merrow could breathe air and survive out of water for about two hours, thus they often came to the surface in search of food.

Combat
Merrow would sometimes attack coastal settlements or "intruding" ships. Using their green coloration, they could hide easily, becoming effectively invisible in oceanic terrain. They attacked from cover, using longspears, and followed up their assault with talons and teeth.

History
The illithid Vestress, queen of Ascarle in 1361 DR, had a standing army with a core of 100 merrow under her command.

Society
Merrow territories usually encompassed an area with a radius between . A chieftain ruled the tribe, and his power-hungry orders dictated the life of the members.
Diet
Merrow were a carnivorous species with voracious appetites, preying on all who entered their territory.
Homelands
Merrow lived in shallow freshwater caves about  below the surface. Some merrow were known to inhabit the Moonsea lake, In Cormanthor, they could be found in the River Ashaba.

Religions
Some merrow worshiped Panzuriel and served him as shamans.
Relationships
Merrow sometimes kept company with scrags.

Notable Merrows
 Tarag

## Meld into stone

Meld into stone was a transmutation spell that caused the caster and his or her gear to merge with stone.

Effects
As mentioned above, meld into stone allowed the caster to meld with stone. This had a number of limitations. First, the caster could only carry up to 100 pounds (45 kg) with him or herself into the stone. Second, the caster was required to choose a stone that was at least as big to completely cover the caster for the one to meld with. Meeting these two conditions was crucial for the spell's success. Not meeting them caused the spell's magic to dissipate without causing anything.

Even when the caster managed to meld inside a stone, the caster was not completely free. He or she was continuously glued with the stone's surface and while the caster could make this connection as small as possible, it was impossible for him or her to get rid of it.

When the stone, inside which the caster was, was destroyed to the extent that it did not meet the aforementioned standard of covering the entire body anymore, the caster was shunted out of the stone, a process that caused physical harm to the caster.

There were a number of spells that were simply dangerous to the caster of meld into stone while within a stone. For example, stone to flesh caused the meld into stone-caster to be shunted out of the stone while suffering physical harm. stone shape caused the one in the stone physical suffering but left him or her inside there, while passwall harmlessly shunted him or her out. The most dangerous spell to be used against the caster of meld into stone was transmute rock to mud. This spell shunted him or her out of the rock and it was potentially lethal to the one who was shunted out.

Components
Meld into stone required somatic and verbal components, but also a divine focus.

History
Meld into stone was used in various areas by various people.

Items
Cloak of stone, a cloak that gave the user the ability to use meld into stone, the construction also required the same spell.

Races
Lith, a chaotic evil race of  that could use the spell as often as they wanted.

Maur, also called hunched giants, they could use the spell three times per day.

Spells
Undermaster, a powerful spell that conferred among others the ability to innately cast meld into stone.

## Message

Message was a transmutation spell that let one converse in whispers at a distance. The spell was language-dependent.

Components
The spell required verbal, somatic and material components. The material required was a short piece of wire.

Inscription
According to the tome known as the Book of the Silver Talon, the ingredients for the ink used to inscribe message into spellbooks were: one ounce of giant squid sepia, a non-human ear, a non-human tongue from a different body than the ear, an angelica plant, a turnip, three shoots of foxtail grass, six leaves of hedge mustard and a drop of dew.

The single drop of dew was gathered from a fern leaf during a full moon and placed dropped in a cauldron of boiling water. The tongue, angelica plant, mustard, foxtail, turnip and ear were shredded and added to the mixture as it heated, in their respective order until it boiled over. The sepia was warmed over a flame, to which was added the residue of the boiling concoction. It was kept on the flame for another hour and stirred constantly. After the hour it was cooled and left in a vial for a full day.

History
The spell was attributed to Netherese arcanist Xanad in  and was originally called Xanad's message.

## Mestil's acid breath

Mestil's acid breath was an arcane spell of the conjuration school of magic practiced by wizards and sorcerers after the .

Effects
The caster breathed out acid in the form of a cone of droplets to a distance of at least 30 feet (9.1 meters), burning all caught within who could not avoid it. It increased in range and potency with the prowess of the caster.

Components
It required verbal, somatic, and material components. The latter was a handful of fire ants, whether dead or living.

## Methil El-Viddenvelp

Methil El-Viddenvelp was an illithid from Phanlinksal who served Yvonnel Baenre, and later Quenthel Baenre, as a secret advisor in the drow city of Menzoberranzan. It was also the founder of the Sept of Ill'Ghact.

Descriptions
Methil was about 6 ft (1.8 m). Its head was slimy and resembled a squid with pure white eyes.

By 1484 DR, Methil had become extremely powerful, existing more in mind than in body. By that point, it was also insane, either from an injury or its great power, or perhaps from both.

As drow using mental magic on each other was considered a grave offense, the Baenre Matron used Methil as a deniable asset to probe her guests' minds, claiming the illithid's negligence and using it as a loophole, as psionics were not magic; and to send untraceable messages to her guests.

History
Background
Methil was originally from the illithid city of Phanlinksal, where it was born from the host of a human sorcerer. It quickly became one of the most influential members of the city. When House Baenre, the ruling house of Menzoberranzan, threatened the illithids, Methil agreed to serve as an advisor to Matron Mother Yvonnel Baenre in order that the settlement be spared. It was also believed that it was a "double agent" keeping tabs on the drow city.

Methil served Matron Baenre as her chief (and secret) advisor on affairs outside the city - as well as providing her with valuable insight on Ruling Council affairs. (She even built a secret room attached to the chamber of the Ruling Council where it could spy on their meetings.) Methil was particularly valuable to Matron Baenre in helping her understand the psionic powers of House Oblodra.

Sept of Ill'Ghact
In 1339 DR, Phanlinksal was destroyed while Methil was still in Menzoberranzan. The next year, Methil founded the Sept of Ill'Ghact, a hidden society of illithids and thralls intent on rebuilding Phanlinksal and restoring the elder brain. Its greatest success was implanting an illithid tadpole in a drow psion from House Oblodra, which subsequently became an ulitharid named Galgast, capable of serving as the foundation for the elder brain.

Injury
In 1358 DR, Methil was involved in the drow attack on Mithral Hall and was believed dead. In truth, it had been badly injured but was rescued (by Bregan D'aerthe) and brought back to the tunnels outside of the city, where it remained for more than a century. Leadership of the Sept of Ill'Ghact eventually passed into the hands of Galgast.

Return
In 1484 DR, Gromph Baenre brought Matron Mother Quenthel Baenre to Methil, who infused her with the memories and indeed some of the personality of their late mother. Quenthel subsequently became much bolder in her actions and planned a Surface conquest for the glory of Lolth. Methil provided the "Darkening" spell to Tsabrak Xorlarrin to aid in Quenthel's plans.

Gromph and Quenthel believed Methil to be quite diminished, but in reality, it was still connected to the illithid hive mind. Kimmuriel Oblodra, who spent a great deal of time with the illithids, was able to secretly gain information from Methil's perspective and discuss it with Jarlaxle.

After Dahlia Sin'felle and Artemis Entreri were captured by the drow in Q'Xorlarrin in 1484 DR, Methil invaded Dahlia's mind, effectively turning her insane and later allowing her to connect with the will of Lolth. He later used his psionic abilities to save Dahlia's life after a tunnel collapse, before turning her over to Gromph and Quenthel. Methil was subsequently used to further Quenthel Baenre's designs on the Silver Marches, including mindwashing the brothers of Thrym to Quenthel's cause.

## Mezzoloth

Mezzoloths, sometimes called mezzodaemons or mezzodemons, were both the most common and lowest-ranking yugoloths. They acted as the wretched grunts in the mercenary legions of the daemons, compensating for their low intelligence with fearsome combat skills.

Description
Mezzoloths appeared as humanoid insects, standing 7 feet (2.1 meters) tall and weighing 250 pounds (113.3 kilograms). They had six lanky limbs, four of which were arms ending in hands tipped with talons. Their entire bodies were covered in dense plates of ivory-colored chitin and their broad skulls were notably armored. When angered, their wide-set, unblinking eyes glared red, a state they were nearly always in. Despite their claws, mezzoloths almost always carried a shield and trident, or two tridents alone, into battle.

Personality
Mezzoloths were simplistic beings driven by the core principles of violence and reward. Beyond these concepts they had little care for, or understanding of, the world around them, spending their time training, imagining new fighting techniques, and wandering the Lower Planes for weak entities to bully. Their lack of intelligence generally forced them to accept their regular abuse, further contributing to their brutality in combat.

Their martial prowess was genuinely fearsome and the creatures delighted in destruction. Despite this, they were still relatively cautious, fleeing from battle when outmatched or outnumbered and unconcerned with honor or duty. They had a reputation as mercenaries, due to their love for treasure and tendency to work alone.

Abilities
Mezzoloths were of a highly magical nature and had a natural affinity for magical items, even those normally restricted by class or alignment. They also possessed extraordinary vision, with their insectoid eyes seeing both the ultraviolet and infrared spectra. They could fight unarmed using their talons and fists to tear their enemies apart. They had access to darkness and dispel magic spells, as well as the power to exude noxious yellow fumes to choke out their foes, while they fought in the fog unimpeded. If pressed they could attempt to call in more mezzoloths to assist them, potentially even hydroloths, or try to teleport away. A limited form of telepathy allowed them to psychically communicate with others so long as they had at least basic sentience.

Combat
When engaging in combat, mezzoloths first tried to soften their prey through their toxic mist before charging in with their weapons, dispelling any magical enchantments they noticed being used. If outmatched, they used their darkness spell to cover their escape and teleporting away. They preferred to use magical weapons and shields in combat but would resort to their powerful claws if none were available. Their group tactics were practically the same, with large squadrons breathing their mist before heading into battle.

Society
Mezzoloths acted as the foot soldiers in the mercenary armies of the yugoloths and the most plentiful members of their kind. Despite being the lowest in rank among the yugoloths, mezzoloths were not the weakest of their race. Yugoloths from greater stations normally ruled over them with ease because of their low intelligence.

Mezzoloths were easily drawn into service on the Material Plane by offering opportunities to destroy or great rewards for their assistance. Their compliance with evil mortals normally depended on the price those beings were willing to pay and whether or not the mortals acknowledged the mezzoloth's "superior" position in the relationship. They commonly associated with night hags, demons, and devils, although they quickly grew weary of the constraining rules and regulations of the devils, but were more likely to establish lairs than rampage across reality like the demons.

Promotion
Yugoloths often had to learn some kind of lesson that was built into their current rank as part of their advancement process. Mezzoloths had been taught that they were the weakest of the daemons and that everyone above them was more powerful, making them fairly easy to bluff, (while this was true of dhergoloths in terms of sheer strength, they were in many ways weaker). By learning and calling out this bluff, often through the direct experience of defeating their dhergoloth "superiors", that being in command didn't always mean having raw strength, mezzoloths could advance to the next stage of power.

Habitat
In the eons since they first spawned, mezzloths grew to populate the lower planes, as well as the Astral and Ethereal Planes. They were particularly common within Gehenna, and were theorized to have had a large portion of their population moved there.

History
The true place where mezzoloths first formed was a point of contention among scholars. They were thought to be the yugoloth adaptations of another monster from the lower planes. Some claimed that the mezzodemons originated within the depths of the Abyss, although later reports did not agree on this.

## Methwood

The Methwood was a dense forest located in the frontier between Chessenta and Unther. After the Spellplague of 1385 DR and until the Second Sundering of the 1480s DR, when the nation of Unther was returned to Toril, the forest was also part of the dragonborn nation of Tymanther.

Geography
Flora & Fauna
The Methwood was a large and hot forest is filled with cypress, date palms, cedar, juniper, and mulberry trees, with lots of vine growth. The vegetation was thick, making travel difficult.

It was home to the common fauna of the Old Empires area, such as elephants, monkeys, and leopards.

Inhabitants
Although the Methwood was the only significant forest in Unther, Untheran people avoided to chop down its trees because of the numerous monsters that inhabited the area.

The Methwood was inhabited mostly by chimeras and Untheran druids, although the druids had either died or left the forest by the 13th century DR. By the late years of the 14th century, the wood was home for small tribes of elves and half-elves. The green dragon Skuthosiin also had a lair there as well, at least before his death in 1479 DR.

In 1374 DR, a cell of the Eldreth Veluuthra, led by Talindra Amalith of the Vel'Nikeryma, operated in the forest.

In the late years of the 15th century, it was discovered that a tribe of fey also lived in the forest. Those fey were hostile against the inhabitants of Luthcheq.

Legends
It was rumored that an ancient city of the Turami who lived in this region before the foundation of Unther was lost in the forest. However, nobody had ever found the slightest hint about the location of this city. Per other legends, the Turami had not built a city, but simply gained access to the Imaskari fortress of Metos inside the forest, staying there for centuries.

Notable inhabitants
 Skuthosiin, the green dragon, had one of his lairs in the Methwood.

## Menzoberranzan/Narbondellyn

Narbondellyn was a district of the drow city of Menzoberranzan.

Geography
The district of Narbondellyn was located next to Narbondel, the city's enchanted clock pillar. A mushroom forest separated it from the plateau of Qu'ellarz'orl.

Description
Narbondellyn was an elegant district with broad streets, fine homes, chic cafes and taverns, and expensive shops. It housed manors, theaters, an arena, and two of the ruling houses.  Multicolored faerie fire highlighted the stone carvings on mansions and illuminated shop signs. It was the home of the wealthy up-and-coming drow houses and no less than seven existing powerful noble houses. Several of the most luxurious and profitable of businesses were located in this neighborhood as well. The best gems and perfumes could be found here, as well as the city's moneylenders.

Inhabitants
Drow were by far the most numerous folk in Narbondellyn. Richly dressed drow shopped and sampled the fares. City guards mounted on large lizards or giant slugs made frequent rounds. Goblins could be found as personal servants, dwarf slaves repaired fine metal items, and minotaurs acted as guards. Kobolds lurked in narrow alcoves and scurried out to clean the streets.

Locations
 House Fey-Branche  One of the ruling houses.
 House Godeep  A minor house of Menzoberranzan.
 House Ousstyl  52nd house in 1372 DR.
 House Srune'Lett The house's compound was in this neighborhood.
 Smithy An enslaved dwarf worked as a smith here in 1361 DR.
 Public bathhouse  The bathhouse had female gnome slaves, as well as goblin slaves.

Notable inhabitants
Gromph Baenre The archmage had a private stalactite manor here. It was a black mansion, carved from stalactites with walls covered in shifting symbols. Residents of the city avoided it.
Liriel Baenre The drow priestess had a miniature castle here.

## Melshimber

The Melshimber family  was a noble house of Waterdeep.

Base of Operations
The family owned a villa in the Sea Ward, on the northwest corner of the Street of Whispers and Grimwald's Way.

Members
 Cynndria Melshimber, Hlanta Melshimber's bard niece from Scornubel.
 Devana Melshimber, formerly Devana Parrantine from Tilverton, sister of Tryola Perrentine, wife of Rivven as of mid-14 century DR.
 Hlanta Melshimber, the matriarch of the family in the mid-14 century DR.
 Khallos Melshimber, Hlanta's heir and the eldest son.
 Rivven Melshimber, a nobleman from Waterdeep, husband to Devana.
Deceased Members
 Camaroon Melshimber

History
In early 1370 DR, Rivven Melshimber married Devana Parrantine of Tilverton. The wedding ceremony was held in Waterdeep but was darkened by the bride's sister, Tryola Perrentine's disappearance. In reality, Tryola was captured by orcs of Horazak's a marauding group that was allied with drow who were plotting Waterdeep's downfall. Several months later, Tryola emerged from Undermountain, alive, traumatized, but not broken. The noblewoman was rescued by an agent of Khelben Arunsun.

In 1371 DR, Lady Hlanta Melshimber was caught in a scandal after her bitter rival, Lady Hyara Talmost, perished under strange circumstances, right after Lady Melshimber publicly embarrassed Hyara by revealing some of the juicy details of the woman's youthful indiscretions. Hyara came back as a ghost and resumed the status of the family's matriarch and accused Hlanta Melshimber of treachery.

## Merith Strongbow

Merith Strongbow  was an elven fighter mage and a member of the Knights of Myth Drannor.  His familiar was a black cat named Shadowclaw.

Description
Merith was tall, standing five feet ten inches. He had dark black hair and one blue colored eye and one green colored eye. He wore both wands and his sword on his belt.

Abilities
In addition to his wizardry, Merith was psionically gifted with the powers detection of good or evil and object reading.

History
Merith was born to Laerune and Lianthorn Strongbow on the banks of the river Duathamper in 1161 DR

## Metamagic

The term metamagic covered a set of techniques, a group of spells, and even magic items that could be used by spellcasters, including wizards, sorcerers, clericss and others, to alter the workings of other spells and magic items. It was considered a sub-school of the school of incantation.

History
An early pioneer in the field of metamagic was Sammaster, when he was but a prodigious young mage, in the early 800s DR.

Metamagic Techniques
 Empower Spell: This increased the intensity of harmful spells.
 Extend Spell: This allowed one to cast a spell with double its usual duration.
 Maximize Spell: This unleashed the full potential of harmful spells.
 Quicken Spell: This allowed spells to be cast quicker.
 Silent Spell: This allowed spellcasting without the necessary verbal components.
 Still Spell: This allowed the caster to cast a spell without the requisite somatic components.

Appendix

## Meazel

Meazels were a murderous, malevolent race of wretched recluses that cared only for wallowing in wistful self-pity. They responded to intrusions on their lonely gloom by killing the offenders or afflicting them with a wicked curse or terrible illness.

Description
Meazels were humanoids that stood between  tall, and weighed approximately . The same meazel could have a skin tone of light gray to dark green, and their dark, greenish-gray hair was waxy and thick. Irregular patches of an angry red hue coated the bodies of 85% of all meazels, caused by a noncontagious skin disease that gave them a leprous and disfigured appearance.

Most meazels had clawed hands and webbed digits that suited the boggy areas they normally occupied, and their sickly skin was contrasted by their jet black eyes. Male and female meazels did both exist, but the two were practically indistinguishable.

Personality
Mean-spirited and malicious, meazels were near universally despised by surface dwellers and underground residents alike. Heinous hermits that they were, they were rarely encountered outside of their foul lairs, and in such cases when they were, it was almost always to inflict suffering. Greedy, cruel, and vindictive, meazels made poor and unreliable allies, being one of the few creatures that near all intelligent subterranean races would work together to combat. Their loathing for other beings extended to other members of their kind, and so the only way to productively interact with a meazel was to make them an unwitting pawn, else convince them to temporarily combat a greater foe.

Meazels themselves were self loathing and filled with bitterness, spending most of their existence contemplating their own terrible lives. Attempting to adopt a young meazel, otherwise known as a whelp, was a fruitless task more likely to backfire than raise a productive member of their patron civilization. The cruelty and callousness of a meazel was so baked into their very essence that raising them to be benevolent was impossible, only resulting in a high functioning psychopath.

Abilities
The original meazels were immune to even the most sickening diseases, and their very presence was practically accursed. A strange curse hung off them, weakening those who stood too close to them. Their very blood carried a dangerous pestilence that caused terrible pain to those they could contaminate via blood to blood contact.

Swamp dwelling meazels had numerous features that helped them to live in their favored environment. Webbed feet allowed them to maneuver the marshlands with ease, and jagged claws let them rip into prey easily, although they still commonly made use of garrote wires. While the original meazels also suffered from the ghastly affliction, their descendants' claws spread their supernatural skin disease to others, causing weakening fortitude and movement. Not every meazel suffered from the physical effects, nor were any of them actually debilitated by it, but it presented them with a deep hunger for humanoid flesh.

Shadowfell meazels were an entirely different brand of creature that lacked many of their signature abilities, although still present was their general build, hatred of others, and use of the garrote wires that undeniably marked them as meazels. Meazels from the Shadowfell lacked the evolved characteristics of the normally swamp dwelling varieties, but instead had powers of their own. The corrupted meazels moved through shadows, both literally and figuratively with ease, stealthily skulking through darkness as well as teleporting through it. Anywhere within  that the meazel had seen before could be instantly arrived at, along with whatever thing or person they had with them at the time. However the effect had a dangerous after effect on the unfortunate victim dragged through the darkness. The curse of a Shadowfell meazel bestowed to their targets was a beacon of darkness that lured nightmarish creatures toward them. Undead and Shadowfell natives within  could sense the mark's location for up to an hour, but by the time the hour had ended many would have already been killed.

Combat
The most iconic weapon utilized by meazels were their garrote wires, used to strangle the unsuspecting humanoids that came across them. They rarely attempted to attack directly, always making use of stealth, or occasionally ranged weapons to kill. Otherwise, they made use of their claws. They preferred to retreat if the odds were not stacked in their favor, and would only engage groups by picking off stragglers.

Society
The miserable meazels despised company and rarely associated with any other intelligent life. Meazels only worked together if stuck in a position of mutual danger and even then they were incredibly untrusting, and untrustworthy. Keeping wild beasts however, was a common occurrence among meazels, such as drakes or spiders to serve guard animals or pets. Meazels with access to some kind of magic may use rituals to summon elementals and bind them to their will. Most often, a meazel encountered with any kind of training was likely to have the skills of a rogue.

If a meazel didn't seek to kill and eat someone they would often simply attempt to steal from them without being noticed, although they looted bodies anyway. A meazel's sense of value was strange and arbitrary in certain ways. Despite being greedy they saw no value in precious stones, only gold, silver, or other types of currency. While they would steal things like armor or weapons even though they couldn't properly use some of them, they did not recognize magical objects as valuable. Although all races hated the meazels, they had traditional enemies in the orcs and kobolds.

Religion
Despite their antisocial nature, meazels were known to work for, maintain relations with, or establish cults dedicated to certain deities. Often these gods were enemies of Baalzebul like Levistus, who promised to rid them of their curse, or deities like Garagos who were dedicated to savage destruction.

Ecology
Meazels normally resided in marshes or subterranean caverns, but they would make their homes anywhere that was damp, dark and cold such as a sewer system. Likewise more shadowy meazels occupied places where the Material Plane and Shadowfell were connected, loitering around crossings to the Plane of Shadow. A meazel's bed was composed of all of their possessions, while anything else such as bone, jewels or magical items, were kept in sacks carelessly thrown around their filthy abodes. Their squalid lairs were overrun with pests and vermin that often made for a quick snack and served to consume anything the meazels didn't want.

Meazels were most commonly found in the Vast Swamp on the eastern border of Cormyr, although they had been known to be located in the sewer systems of some cities. Beyond Toril, meazels could be found on the world of Athas.

Diet
Although meazels could subsist on other types of meat, their ravenous hunger could only be sated by consuming the flesh of sentient humanoids, goblinoids, and kobolds.  Normally they dragged their killed prey back to their lairs in order to safely consume them.

Reproduction
When the infrequent urge to mate crept its way into their minds, meazels females would give birth to one or two whelps, who became independent at three years old and fully matured at six. Mating was one of only two reasons why a meazel would generally seek out another of its kind (the other being to unite against a powerful foe).

History

The first meazels were created in ages past during a terrible plague that practically destroyed a city in the resulting rage and chaos of the outbreak. Prayers, magic, and conventional healing methods could do nothing to cure the ailment blighting the civilization. The plague inflicted terrible and near infinite hunger and could kill most people within a few days. When the suffering caused by the disaster was at its peak, a soothing and intangible voice made an offering to the afflicted. Those starved by the plague would be given access to a limitless food supply, and no longer would the people be killed by it. This deal with the devil turned out to be truer than most suspected, as the ominous benefactor who had made the offer was actually Baalzebul, the Duke of the Seventh Hell, Maladomini.

Whether or not the victims were made aware of the archdevil's nature and simply were too tortured to care was unknown. In true devil fashion Baalzebul was despicably true to his word, preventing the plague from killing those afflicted, but leaving them with the sores and hunger that were its symptoms. The first half of their deal was also upheld, but also corrupted in the cruelest of ways. Like ghouls or gnolls, normal food could hardly sate the unquenchable need to consume, and only the flesh of sentient humanoids could satisfy a meazel's hunger. This revelation was found after the original meazels searched far and wide for the respite from the hunger they were promised.

It would appear that Baalzebul's curse was not unique, or at least was a unique variant on a type of corruption. Meazels of a different quality could be made without interference from the Lord of Lies, although their powers were drastically different. Shadowfell meazels were created when, in an attempt to escape the miseries of their previous existence, embittered individuals entered the Plane of Shadow and were warped by its influence. The intense negative emotions they harbored when first traveling to the Shadowdark eventually twisted them, staining their souls with a powerful dark energy.

In the mid-to-late 14 century DR, an organized group of meazels had formed in the sewers beneath Westgate, and would attack the residents with fierce savagery. It was rumored that they might have been a cult dedicated to Garagos, or otherwise some kind of brutal thieves' guild.

## Metatext

The Metatext was the supposed metaphysical text which was said to grant access to all the secrets of the multiverse. It was considered to be a sacred idealization of the church of Deneir and was the magnum opus of their collective scholarly work.

Nature
According to Deneiran doctrine, the Metatext manifested in the mortal realms in every text or written document that was ever produced.

Powers
It was believed that by reading the Metatext, a mortal individual could ascend to godhood.

History
Legend stated that the mortal Deneir ascended to divinity, and took on the role of Oghma's deific right hand.

In the, the goddess Mystra was assassinated by Cyric, with the aid of Shar. Upon the goddess' death, the Weave ― which allowed for the use and manipulation of magic ―  began to collapse. Deneir attempted to stabilize the Weave by writing himself and the Metatext into the Weave, but was believed to have been destroyed in the process.

At some point, the Deneiran scholar Iosefa Elgin attempted to recreate the Metatext in the tome Metatext: Rebound. For that heretical action she was excommunicated from the Deneiran church.
