## Meriadar

Meriadar was the ever-patient deity of the mongrelfolk.

Description
Meriadar was said to be able to appear as a tall member of any goblinoid species, but more often he appeared as an  mongrelfolk with elf-like ears, a dwarven nose, a human chin, an orcish jaw, and gnome-like eyes. His exact height, weight, build, and color changed moment to moment, however. He always wore plain brown robes.

Personality
Meriadar was a peaceful, but not pacifistic, and orderly deity.

Abilities
As part of his peaceful nature, Meriadar used protective magics, such as wards, with great skill and cunning.

His avatars, though largely peaceful, were known to use power word, stun, power word, blind, Otiluke's resilient sphere, Otiluke's telekinetic sphere, and forcecage to subdue any being foolhardy enough to attack him. His avatars also typically carried a wand of size alteration for the same purpose. He fought with a powerfully enchanted quarterstaff that affected any goblinoid struck as if by a symbol of persuasion.

Divine Realm
Meriadar lived on the plane of Arcadia, and the town of Ghetto in that plane was said to be a splinter of his realm and under his protection. His realm, the Hand of Peace, was located on Buxenus.

Activities
Meriadar was known to send out his avatars for defensive purposes, but omens from him were far more common. Such omens were known to take the form of automatic speech, speaking in tongues during philosophical debates, sudden artistic inspiration (usually coupled with automatic drawing or sculpting), bubbles in bowls of soup that released smoky, vaporous symbolic images, and strange scents that alerted mongrelfolk to imminent danger.

Relationships
Meriadar, though a good deity, had long spurned the aid of other good deities in his attempts to draw non-evil goblinoids to his worship, as he did not wish to alienate such potential worshipers by allying himself with the deities of their enemies.

The various goblinoid deities had an enmity for Meriadar, and he himself had a particular antipathy for the bugbear deities.

Worshipers
Meriadar was primarily worshiped by the mongrelfolk of whom he was the patron, though he was also known to be worshiped by non-evil goblinoids.

The priesthood of Meriadar preached peace, but they also helped to defend mongrelfolk communities, as well as acted as advisors to leaders concerning social policy. They supported laws, hierarchies, and established leaders unless those were grossly unjust.

Meriadar's symbol, a decorated bowl, held great significance as an object of both artistic and practical purposes, combining the two into one. Such bowls were used for both sacramental feasting and the sharing of food. His priests and shamans were also known to use the decorated bowl symbol as a starting point for philosophical discussion, as they "debate the quality of the 'eternal now' as having its origins in spiritual parallels to the act of drawing sustenance, and the immediacy (and 'nowness') of the most basic earthly drive, that of hunger." Meriadar smiled on such philosophical and spiritual thought, sometimes of great nuance, occurring amongst his priests despite their strongly orderly tendencies.

Meriadar had two known proxies: Feryli Krenurum in Nemausus, and Magruntel Dent-head in Arcadia.

Rumors & Legends
Some sages thought that Meriadar was responsible for the creation of the buseni.

## Metal shape

Metal shape was a divine magic spell granted to priests of Flandal Steelskin, Lord of Smiths. It allowed the caster to form a piece of ordinary metal into any desired shape within limits.

Effects
This spell required a model made from lead, hammered into roughly the desired shape by the caster, as the material component. Metal shape could affect up to  (even larger for more experienced casters) of ordinary (non-magical) metal and produce an implement (such as a tool or weapon), an object (such as a door or a statue), or a container (such as a coffer or an urn). However, if the intended shape was supposed to have a sharp edge, there was only a moderate chance that it would be keen enough to cut. And if the target item had moving parts, there was a moderate chance they didn't work. The caster had to touch the metal to be reshaped in order to cast this spell, and the change was instantaneous.

Another advantageous use of metal shape was to cast it upon metal before it was worked by a smith into its final form. Used in this way, it cut the time and cost to craft a finished piece by at least half.

Components
This spell required verbal and somatic components, and a small model made from lead, hammered into shape by the caster.

## Melon pie

Melon pie was a rarely-liked type of pie pastry stuffed with melon, predominately found in Amn and the city of Westgate.

History
As of the late 14 century DR, melon pie was a rare sight indeed. It was served at the Harfin Draether eatery and tavern of Athkatla's Temple District, where the "treat" could be seen sitting rotting away, plastered with flies. Despite its "appetizing" appearance, this pie was often purchased not for its taste but rather because it was served alongside a plateful of universally beloved sugar-cookies. Despite most folk's apparent aversion to the dish, it still had its fans, notably Ruldan of Westgate, a man famed for his proclivities, love for melon pies, and sculpting the Lady Rapturous, dedicated to Sune – the goddess of love.

## Melairrin

Melairrin was an ancient dwarven citadel that sat in the heart of Mount Sar in the Sword Mountains north of Waterdeep.

Description
The ancient dwarfhold was long abandoned, and consisted of both a citadel and adjoining caverns. It connected to the exterior of Mount Sar via western tunnels that led through the abode of the frost giant Harshnag, a member of the Gray Hands. The lower reaches of the hold were also accessible via a portal known as the Sundered Throne in the Dungeon Level of Undermountain, although reports differed as to whether this was a one- or two-way gateway.

History
The fortress was claimed by the orcs of Uruth Ukrypt shortly after the founding of their realm in the, and provided them a secure base from which to wage wars on the surrounding human and gnome settlements of the Sword Coast North and Dessarin Valley. It also served as the seat of the realm's political power where the Bloodbone Throne of Uruth was kept.

During the Rage of Dragons in the, the black dragon Shammagar attacked Melairrin and claimed it from the orcs as his lair. Three centuries later, much of his hoard was famously plundered by Asilther Graelor, a wood elf thief and Harper. Wary of further thefts, Shammagar abandoned the citadel to seek more remote lairs in the middle of the Trackless Sea and to take revenge on Asilther.

Inhabitants
As of the mid-to-late 14 century DR, Melairrin was home to a variety of monsters that had arrived from the caverns of the Underdark beneath it, as well as to bands of half-dragon orcs believed to be descended from Shammagar. The deepest depths of the fortress hosted the lair of Rorrina Stoneshaft, a dwarven vampire who served Artor Morlin of Waterdeep.

## Megaraptor

Megaraptor was a species of dinosaurs, larger and fiercer cousin of a deinonychus that shared their habitats and behavior.

Description
Megaraptors were large and lean bipedal dinosaurs with wicked-looking claws on their feet. Just like their smaller cousins, megaraptors' hides were bright, reminiscent of the tropical bird plumage in color. Usual hide colors included bright green, orange, or red along the back and flanks, with the lighter shade of the same color on the belly. Megaraptors' backs usually had dark spots or stripes. An average specimen stood  tall and could reach  in length.

Abilities
Even at an advanced age, or if blinded, megaraptors remained deadly predators, thanks to their extremely keen sense of smell. Being strong animals, megaraptors could carry as much as .

Behavior
Megaraptors could be encountered as solitary hunters as well as packs of as many as six dinosaurs. Their brains were relatively big for a dinosaur, and they showed cunning and pack-hunting instincts.

Combat
When attacking, megaraptors used their speed and strong legs to charge and pounce at their victims. When in melee rage, the dinosaurs used their sharp teeth, talons, and foreclaws.

Ecology

Megaraptors were known to inhabit the jungles of Samarach on the Chultan Peninsula and in the Malatran Plateau of Kara-Tur.

Similar to deinonychuses, megaraptors were trainable animals. Goblins, hobgoblins, and troglodytes were known to use them as mounts. However, they were a rare sight as megaraptors were hard to control. A living megaraptor egg could fetch a price of 13,000 gp, while the cost of training of a single creature was valued at 2,000 gp.

There were known cases of successful megaraptor and red dragon breeding producing half-dragon mounts used by githyanki. Thankfully, such breeds were not known to exist on Toril.

Megaraptors could be magically summoned through the summon nature's ally VI spell and fiendish megaraptors – via summon monster VII. Magically reanimated megaraptors were known to be used to create revived fossils, zombies, and skeletons.

History
In the, megaraptors were a common threat to the gnomes operating the illegal logging operation a the Lantan-Samarach portal. One such beast made the engineers' lives complicated after it swallowed a mechanism essential to the portal's operation. The device was designed to remain functional even in the roughest of environments and could simply be extracted after the dinosaur's death.

## Merrik

History
In the, the Zhentarim became targeted by a mysterious Order of the Crippled Fang. The Shind Road that connected Hillsfar to the rest of the Moonsea region became swarmed by the fiends in service to the cult. The creatures relentlessly attacked Zhentarim patrols and agents. Merrik and his fellow men were among those who stood against the fiends, and a shadow dragon Gloomfang, leaving him bloodied, battered, and hurt but alive. The Zhent fighter reached the safety of Yûlash only to be arrested by Haliator Mendara and his Red Plumes as an enemy to the Red Plumes. Haliator toyed with the idea of executing Merrik where he stood but decided to take the Zhent prisoner, heal his wounds and have him stand trial in Yûlash.

## Merchant's scale

A merchant's scale, or balance, was an appraising tool used to measure the weight of items.

Description
This small item consisted of balanced pans. Appropriate standardized weights were placed on one pan and the item being measured was placed in the other. If the pans remained balanced, the item being appraised shared the same weight as the sum of the standard weights. Merchant's most often used the scales to assure that precious metals were not counterfeits, but could be used to confirm the value of any item sold by weight.

A typical set would include an assortment of sizes of pans and weights. The scale usually weighed about  and was sold for about 2 gold pieces in most economies. Aurora's Emporium sold an especially accurate, tabletop model intended for wizards' laboratories for 30 gold pieces. This set was advertised as being able to measure the weight of cricket's leg, using standard weights from  to .This is false advertising; the smallest weight in the set is more massive than the weight of an entire cricket, let alone one's leg. Crickets weigh less than a gram.

Reputation
The merchant's scale was often used as a symbol of moral or ethical balance or of justice. For example, the scale was used in the iconography of the god Tyr, and the Church of Tyr even contained specialty priests known as scales. The god Amaunator sometimes manifested as a woman bearing the image of a scale on her dress.

The Ravenian Strangestars House had a merchant's scale featured on their coat of arms, flanked on both sides by an arc of stars.

Firbolgs used scales to cast votes within their tribes.

No wizard's lab was complete without a set of scales.

Notable Scales
 Balance of Belaros  A relic sacred to the god Tyr, which had magically hovering scale pans.

## Mendryll Belarod

Mendryll Belarod was a young half-elven bard who traveled Faerûn widely in the 14 century DR, getting into misadventures and writing accounts of the people and places he encountered.

Description
Mendryll had long blonde hair and green eyes. He wore a perpetual smirk.

Personality
He was a gregarious and charming person, and got increasingly cocky and bold with the opportunity to show off. He was very flirtatious with women when given the chance.

He claimed to find Cormyr boring and limiting due to its stable and lawful nature, though it didn't stop him from enjoying himself there.

Abilities

Despite appearing a dandyish wastrel, he was a proficient swordsman and was perfectly capable of protecting himself.

His bardic abilities tended towards the defensive and utilitarian, to better assist his travels and keep himself alive.

He possessed the mundane yet specialized talents of being able to belch the Cormyrean Royal Anthem and impersonate famous people such as Elminster and Piergeiron Paladinson.

Possessions
As a frequent traveler who found or made trouble wherever he went, Mendryll was well-prepared for dangerous situations. To keep himself alive, he used bracers of defense, a powerful ring of protection, a flame tongue longsword, and a harp of charming. He also had a Bucknard's everfull purse, a regular harp, and a riding horse.

History
In 1367 DR, he wrote a travelogue detailing his exploration of the Moonsea, repeatedly getting into trouble as he explored each city. He earned a potential enemy in Thentia when he unintentionally ruined the elven maiden Elaesse Loridiel's relationship with her unicorn.

In the, he appeared at King Azoun IV's birthday party at Thistleflame Keep in Suzail with, providing music and entertainment for the event. In the process of singing a shockingly song about two Sembites, a dryad, and a harpy, he terribly offended a group of Sembite adventurers, the Stag Runners, who ambushed him later that night. Managing to escape the attack, he then became entangled in a murder and kidnapping plot by the Emerald Swords company, but was exonerated by having the alibi of spending the night with the Purple Dragon officer Aleka Ravenheart (though she would publicly deny any such event occurred).

Later that year, he found himself in the Stonelands pursuing rumors involving the Four from Cormyr, Tower of Ruin, and the Starburst Swords. Hoping to get some new tales out of it, he pointed a group of adventurers towards the likely locations with the promise of fabulous treasure. He then realised the scale and danger of the adventure he had just prompted, and brought along Rhyn Mournsoul, who sought a tragic and hopefully painless death. Meeting the adventurers at the ruins of Darkenshield, he fought alongside them against Lord Vorik Aris's small army.

## Mercykiller ring

Mercykiller rings were enchanted rings worn by the members of the Mercykillers of the planar city of Sigil.

Powers
The wearers of these rings received its magical boons that included enhanced abilities to hide in shadows, to move silently, and to set traps.

History
Mercykiller rings were handed out to the assassins of the Mercykillers who were tasked with ending lives of those who broke laws of Sigil. These Mercykiller assassins were known to be dispatches across the multiverse, including the Prime Material plane.

While it was unknown how a mercykiller ring appeared on Toril, it came into the possession of a merchant named Deidre, a member of Aurora's Magic Emporium, during the 14 century DR. Sometime prior to the  she began selling her wares in the Adventurer Mart of Athkatla.

## Merialeth Loriamanthor

Merialeth "Merry" Loriamanthor was the elven owner of Merry's Mounts and More in Halfhap in the mid-to-late 14 century DR.

Description
Merry was a fair elven maid.

Personality
Merry had a deep love of animals, and greatly valued their welfare.

History
Around 1369 DR, she operated her stables in Halfhap. It was known that she was interested in finding a romantic partner of the elven, human, or half-elven variety, with the stipulations that they respected animals and weren't a druid.

At one point, Merry was harassed by a man while having dinner at the Marching Myrmidon, who suggested that the "and More" of her stables included certain disreputable services. He followed her home, continuing his harassment, until Merry reached home and shot him through the eye with her longbow. She was not arrested.

Relationships
She was the daughter of Melkeras, a wealthy merchant and merchant guildmaster.

At age 19, she was a brown-haired and blue-eyed.

Insightful and good-natured, young Melisana was wise enough to avoid being spoiled by her overindulgent parents, and had decided to take over Melkeras's business whenever he decided to retire from its day-to-day management. ## Methild's harp

Methild's harp was a magical harp used by the Harpers.

Powers
Methild's harp had the ability to part all webs, open all locks, break all bonds, and untie all knots within a  radius of wherever it was played. Ropes of constriction, ropes of entanglement and rugs of smothering ceased functioning, releasing any trapped creatures. If a creature was currently trapped by said items, they were unable to play the harp to gain their freedom.

However, magical barriers such as protective symbols, forcecages, shields, walls of force that do not have an opening cannot be affected by the harp. Additionally, there was a chance that magical knots and locks could become immune to the effects of Methild's harp forever. Objects that were successfully affected by the harp's power were outlined by a glowing orange faerie fire from the moment they were affected.

History
Methild's harp originated from the ancient city of Myth Drannor. However, a legend talked about the bard named Methild who lived in a tiny valley kingdom of Chilldion far from civilization. One winter night, a coven of three hags took over the valley after ensorcelling the royal family. The hags were planning on eating the family whilst they were immobilized by the foul holding magics. After a long day of conquest, spellcasting, and slaughtering of the innocent, the coven grew tired and ordered Methild, the court bard, to strum them a song. The bard was loyal to his king and brought along his enchanted harp. He played the instrument and focused its magics on the king first, dissolving the holding spell and allowing the king to sneak away. Methild continued to play and remove magic from everyone restrained by the hags, one by one. When every victim was freed, Methild could play no longer, and the coven realized the treachery. The bard did not escape and became the hags' supper instead of the king's family.

Appendix
See Also
 A Scroll of the Harpers let users transform their pins into Methild's harp and various other instruments from Myth Drannor, if only for a short time.

## Memories of Queen Fee

Memories of Queen Fee was an expensive club found in Suzail, Cormyr around the . It was named after the
past Cormyrean queen, Filfaeril Obarskyr.

Location
The club overlooked the Promenade in Suzail.

Atmosphere
The club was considered the most fashionable and expensive of clubs that held a view of the Promenade.

Nobles could be found here, trading drinks and gossip alike. Idle and rich nobles were particularly eager, wishing to participate in the most recent and juiciest of gossips. Evenings here would often involve heavy partaking of alcohol, leading patrons to often dine and drink at home in advance.

Exclusive upper rooms were available to patrons.

History
Memories of Queen Fee had been the host to many esteemed guests, ranging from several Cormyrean nobles, to Mirt and the Vampire Manshoon. Rumour mongering asides, the club was the site of veiled threats between its patrons.

It was also here, that Manshoon was finally caught by War Wizards with Mirt's assistance.

## Menga

Menga was a variety of bush that could be found in the jungles of Chult. The leaves of the menga bush had healing properties.

Description
Leaves of the menga plant were thin, long, and green in color and composed of fine fibers.

Ecology
Menga bushes were a rarity in the Chultan jungle.

Uses
To access the healing properties of menga leaves, they had to be dried for at least a day before being ground up, dissolved in heated liquid, and ingested. It took about  to confer any benefits, which were mild relative to most magical healing. Ingesting more than  in a day could render a creature unconscious for up to an hour. Ahealthy menga bush had enough leaves to produce up to  of dried leaves.

In addition, the long and fine fibers of the leaves could be woven into menga cloth, which was used for everything from fabric to awnings to ships' sails.

History
The druid Arvo of Ravens Bluff noted the curative properties of menga leaves in the mid-to-late 14 century DR.

Menga bushes were grown in the gardens of Nangalore and could be found in Omu.

As of the late 15 century DR, the sale of plants such as menga in Port Nyanzaru was governed by the merchant prince Jessamine. An ounce of dried menga leaves sold for 2 gold pieces in Chult while a bolt of menga cloth could cost 50 gold pieces.

## Metsubishi

A metsubishi was a weapon unique to the continent of Kara-Tur.

Description
A metsubishi was a compact wooden device that expelled a burst of powder directly toward a target's face. It consisted of a mouthpiece situated at one end, a chamber designed to hold the powder, and a small tube located at the opposite end. By forcefully exhaling through the mouthpiece, the powder was propelled through the tube, potentially affecting the target's face. Commonly used substances included pepper, ashes, and dust. This device found frequent utilization among ninjas for the purpose of diversion and escape, as well as by city constables in their endeavors to apprehend criminals.

## Memory Spire

The Memory Spire, also known as the Phaerimm Memory Spire, was a mysterious silver tower located in the Anauroch region that held the key to the survival and eventual return of the phaerimm in the late 15 century DR.

Location
The tower was known to appear and disappear irregularly throughout the northern Anauroch (within the territory of the returned Empire of Netheril as of the 15 century DR). The Shadovar of Netheril believed that there existed a pattern to its appearances, and hoped to discover this pattern in order to predict its future locations.

Interior
The Memory Spire was a tomb-like structure that housed a whole hive of phaerimm in stasis. In addition, it contained many phaerimm-made magical weapons designed specifically to destroy the Netherese and to kill Shadovar.

Defenses
The Memory Spire was protected both by the fact that it seemed to move about at random, but also by powerful traps and guardians.

History
Following the Return of Netheril in the, the Netherese made it a priority to exterminate the phaerimm, their ancient nemeses. By the late 15 century DR, the Netherese believed that they had successfully not only wiped out the phaerimm, but also had incinerated all of their corpses to ensure that they would never return. However, they remained concerned about the mysterious Memory Spire, which they surmised to be a tomb containing at least one phaerimm corpse alongside a presumed trove of anti-Netheril magic, weapons, and lore. They thus sought a way to track the location of the Spire in order to destroy it.

By the, Netheril had all but collapsed following the destruction of its capital, Thultanthar, and had not yet managed to destroy the Memory Spire. During this year, the remainder of the Netherese forces battled a Bedine uprising over control of the Memory Spire, and the fighting awoke the hive of phaerimm within. They soon unleashed the same life draining magic that the phaerimm had once used to create the Anauroch desert long ago, and again laid waste to the lands of Netheril.

While the phaerimm of the Spire had been reawakened, they remained trapped inside the tower by the grace of Mystra. In the years following the Second Sundering, they were monitored by weaveghosts who had the power to foil any phaerimm escape attempts by manipulating the Weave itself. Nevertheless, they were still able to enact their life-draining magics and, slowly but surely, had found ways to cast other spells as well.

## Meruyama

Meruyama was a volcano located in Kozakura.

Description
Meruyama was located in Migri Province in the cool northern region of the island of Shinkoku. This slumbering volcano occasionally rumbled with restlessness, holding the distinction of being the loftiest among all of Kozakura's volcanoes. Surprisingly, its slopes remained unusually warm, seldom retaining snow, even during the coldest winters. The peak intermittently emitted steam and sporadically released ash and mud flows, contributing to numerous earthquakes in the surrounding area.

Inhabitants
The presence of tengu and oni around Meruyama could be particularly vexing, creating a bothersome environment for travelers and locals alike. Furthermore, encounters with shinen-gaki were not uncommon.

## Mensel

Mensel of Cormyr was a nobleman and a Lord of Immersea, who ruled many years before the .

History
In life, Lord Mensel was known as a kind, generous, and humanitarian-to-a-fault nobleman. He was universally beloved by his family, servants, and subjects. Mensel died of old age in his sleep.

Later, however, his family discovered a secret vault underneath the Lord's study. It was filled with a small hoard of treasure that was claimed by Mensel's heirs. One item of note in the hoard was the book called the Obscene Grimoire. The tome and its past owners remained largely a mystery, and a century after Lord Mensel's death, countless rumors and legends grew around the sinister black tome.

One rumor of note came from an Arabelan seer, who claimed that Lord Mensel fashioned the Obscene Grimoire for personal use at great expense and ordered everyone involved with the book's creation murdered. Despite this story's dubious authenticity, the legend nevertheless damaged Mensel's name and his family's reputation.

## Merchants' map

A merchants' map was a kind of production and transportation flowchart used by crafters and traders in the Realms, quite different from the typical kind of map that adventurers might be interested in.

Description

A merchants' map was a document that diagrammed and detailed each step in the manufacture of a particular item, from the raw ingredients to the final product. Many products such as candles, glass vials, lanterns, and wares and tools such as axes had short and simple maps describing them, while expensive jewelry had a lengthy and complex series of steps involved. Some production lines had multiple discrete stages within the same business and location, such as an iron mine having its own bloomery where it smelted and cast metal wares.

These maps were highly valued by enterprising traders and mercantile companies who took part in the mass-production and bulk transport of goods, as they gave a vital overview of the flow of goods and services across the length and breadth of Faerûn. With a merchants' map, trade costers enabled their large-scale operations by planning out the routes and supplies involved, and pre-calculated the required wagons, drovers, beasts of burden, warehousing, and other expenses. In addition to these necessary tasks, traders used the maps to eliminate movements of wagons with empty cargo beds, instead taking up the opportune transportation of non-perishable goods in between their own endeavors.

The worth of a merchants' map lay not just in the profitable operations of merchant costers. Opportunistic thieves who came across one could use it as a framework for planning robberies and heists, while more level-headed militants would find profit in knowing where to find steady work guarding warehouses and caravans. Those who had an especially long view (such as retired adventurers) could leverage the details to provide necessary services to the merchants such as warehouses, fortified waystations, and a trusted mercenary service when needed. Savvy factors and investors (and opportunistic interlopers) used merchants' maps to find inefficiencies and weaknesses in the long chain of production that could be addressed (or exploited), earning huge amounts of coin with the stroke of a pen. The most famous and extreme example of this was Thaelon Morgyr, who used his far-sighted intelligence to refine hundreds of merchants' maps, which gave his own businesses a distinct advantage over others and ruthlessly found weaknesses in the competition. At the time of his death, he was estimated to have assets in excess of 16,000,000 gold pieces in value.

Arthane Longswords
An example of an above-average-complexity merchants' map was that of the Arthane longswords, a common "mid-range" blade sold in bulk in Waterdeep and exported all across the North and Western Heartlands. The longswords originated in the Arthane Furnace and were distributed at the Arthane Armory, but a great number of other independent individuals and businesses were involved in the process to turn them from naked blades to finished ones.

 Arthane Furnace forged the basic iron blades near Beliard, using charcoal and iron ore from Arthane-owned properties.
 Maraszymo Darluth harvested and dried blueleaf and duskwood for hilt grips.
 Anastur's Abrasives supplied special polishing powders made from various plant oils and minerals.
 Gleskur's Finemetals of Secomber produced fine braided copper wire for securing hilt wrappings, with the metal sourced from secret dwarven contacts.
 House of Yarthin provided whetstones from Ironmaster for Samril's to sharpen the raw blades with.
 Galdegut's Boneyard supplied bone for carving into pommels.
 Falthryn and Daughters Tannery of Lannort provided cured hides for wrapping hilts.
 Sorkryn's Soft Hides of Scornubel also provided hides.
 Samril's Fine Blades assembled and fitted the hilts to the longsword tangs, polished and sharpened the blades, and packed them for transport to Waterdeep. They sourced materials from minor local suppliers when necessary, and also made their own sealant gum and forged brass pommels.
 Famryn's Fine Oils of Baldur's Gate provided secret oils used in the final sharpening and treatment at the Armory.
 House of Yarthin also provided the whetstones used at the Armory to give the blades their last pre-sale sharpening.
 Whelanxdryn's of Elturel provided scabbards to the Armory, made using their own in-house tanners and blacksmiths.
 The Arthane Armory in Waterdeep put the final touches on the blades by sharpening and scabbarding them, and presented them for sale to the public.

## Megapede

Megapedes were gigantic, centipede-like creatures that could be found in deserts and other sandy environments.

Description
They had extremely long, segmented bodies that were covered in coarse, bristly hairs. At birth, a megapede measured around  in length, and grew to its full size within three months. On average, a full sized megapede measured around  in length. Hundreds of pairs of legs extended from either sides of the body, each measuring  and tipped with flexible claws. There was one pair of legs every .

The head of each megapede featured enormous, powerful mandibles.

Abilities
Megapedes possessed tremorsense, which allowed them to detect anything else underground within a range of .

Combat
A megapede buried itself beneath the surface of sand, or within rocky caverns, waiting to strike out against any unawares creature that would become its next meal.

While the poisonous bite of a megapede could be quite lethal, its most potent weapon was the psychic bomb it employed. Each megapade exuded an aura of necrotic energy that drained the life of nearby creatures, and made them susceptible to a unique psychic attack that would incapacitate them.

Ecology
Diet
The megapede omnivorous species, capable of surviving on vegetation alone for weeks. The favorite prey of the megapedes of Athas were herds of erdlus and kanks.
Habitats
These creatures inhabited warm, desert environments. They were most notably found on the harsh desert world of Athas, where they were counted among the most dangerous creatures to encounter.

History
The mercane Vocath was known to employ a megapede named Queen Gorma in the arena of his personal domain in wildspace.

## Merrig

Description
Merrig was an old human man with hair whitened by age long before its time. His face was rugged and weathered by the sea and sun. Despite his intimidating look, Merrig was a jolly man with a wide, welcoming smile that hid his hardened inner steel. He was also said to be able to bring an ogre under the table.

History
By the, Merrig was already an aged man. He could often be found in Westgate's Black Eye Tavern, drinking ale and telling mariner stories. When questioned, Merrig claimed to be a "pirate hunter," but he was open to any lucrative business partnerships. He did not seem to care about the city guard finding out about his illicit operations.

## Melancholy

Melancholy, or depression, was an affliction of a mind characterized by a number of symptoms, including loss a will to live, loss of interest, self-loathing, withdrawal from the world, loss of will and proactivity, and sometimes occasional fits of anger.

Acquisition
Melancholy was a common affliction that affected humans, dragons, kopru, and even undead. It could be caused by mundane events in one's life, like the loss of the loved ones, solitude, loss of status or magical prowess, or trauma.

In some cases, depression and lethargy was an effect of a malevolent spell such as emotion, Sorrel's dirge, Xult's magical doom, or a magical despair song of a night twist.

Treatment
Melancholy was an affliction that required attention of a professional. Some of these healers of the mind were known to operate in Faerûn, namely, in the city of Baldur's Gate. However, some individuals who suffered from the affliction were resistant to the idea of seeking therapy.

Some believed that certain gemstones could protect the wearers from various afflictions, including melancholy. Such stones included black coral, garnet, lapis lazuli, ruby, and sardonyx.

Some chose to remedy melancholy by erasing their dark memories with the use of the Church of Shar, while others embraced the goddess' teaching fully, fueled by depression.

Notable Affected
 Aribeth de Tylmarande, paladin of Tyr, suffered depression after the unjust execution of Fenthick Moss, which made her an easy target for Morag's manipulations.
 Arrion Weatherspoon, a member of the Fellowship of Bards of Ravens Bluff in the late 14 century DR.
 Arveiaturace, once a servant steed of Meltharond Thone, she eventually fell in love with her master and after his death, she fell into a state of deep depression in 1326 DR.
 Chardath Spulzeer, a noble of the Spulzeer, who fell into deep melancholy following the death of his sister circa 1353 DR.
 Daen Winterbrush, a famed wielder of The Sword of Myrloch Vale, who was overcome with melancholy after witnessing the evils of the world and retreated to live with the fae of the Myrloch Vale. Elrl Rauthantannar, the The Whisperer in Shadows and a Magister of Mystra, who suffered melancholy caused by loneliness.
 Evaine, a sorceress from Phlan who dedicated her second life to destruction of the pools of radiance in the mid-to-late 14 century DR.
 Gyldro Angleiron, the master smith of Rivington General, who suffered from loneliness and broken-heart-related melancholy in the late 15 century DR.
 Lauzoril, the 14 century Zulkir of Enchantment from Thay suffered from fits of melancholy for many years. Those around him often called it "the enchanter temperament."
 Lorsan, an elf trapped in Nalavara's hoard demiplane, dealt with his crushing despair and misery by using self-harm to distract himself from the situation.
 Saed, the vampire Beast Chieftain of Veldorn, who was consumed by melancholy after loosing fortune and power.
 Wulfgar, one of the Companions of the Hall who suffered from depression following his traumatic experiences circa'' 1369 DR.
 Xan of Evereska may have been chronically depressed, or he may have simply been extraordinarily sarcastic, or both.

## Mercykiller's belt

The Mercykiller's belt was a unique enchanted belt once worn by the factol Dom of Sigil circa the early 10 century DR.

Description
The Mercykiller's belt was a thick and wide leather belt crafted using the hide of a creature unknown on Toril. It was bound in polished copper strips. The belt front center was decorated with a circle of polished cream-colored malachite carved into an emblem showing a winged serpent with its maw open and fangs facing outward – the symbol of the Mercykillers. The malachite was affixed in place with copper loops running between layers of the alien hide. The belt buckled in the back with hefty steel clasps.

Powers
The Mercykiller's belt significantly boosted the wearer's physical strength.

History
Sages discovered the mysterious belt's history stretching all the way to the . It was created circa that year in the extra-planar city of Sigil to be worn by factol Dom, a Prison warden for the Mercykillers faction. Dom gained infamy after being a part of the Great Break event organized by the Free League of Sigil – the only mass Prison break in the penitentiary's long history. The factol was held responsible for the grand failure, stripped of his rank, and convicted to spend five years in the very same Prison. After release, Dom spent thirty years tracking down all escapees and bringing them to justice.

By the age of sixty-seven, Dom was still on his quest, but the age was taking its toll. The Mercykiller's belt was fashioned to boost the man's failing strength. Three years later, the last of the criminals were brought to justice. Dom reported to the Prison's new warden, filled all the needed paperwork out, and died less than an hour later from exhaustion.

By some unknown means, the Mercykiller's belt found its way to Toril, where, in the, it belonged to the mind flayer Oinchack'Olp in the Underdark tunnels under the Spine of the World and Icewind Dale. The belt might've been rewarded to a group of mercenaries from Targos, who stood against the Legion of the Chimera in 1312 DR.

## Medallion of proof

The medallion of proof was an enchanted amulet and changed its appearance depending on which deity the wearer drew powers from, worshipped, or was aligned with.

Powers
The medallion of proof radiated a strong aura of alteration magic. Its transformation was not a conscious decision of the wearer but was guided by their true feelings and alignment. In a case when the wearer venerated several deities, the medallion of proof changed to reflect the god that the wearer was most aligned with. On rare occasions when the wearer had no power they worshiped – the amulet turned into a blank disk. The medallion of proof's magic could not be blocked by spells nor other enchanted items, and the medallion could not be used as a holy symbol by paladins nor clerics.

When the amulet represented Chauntea, it depicted a flower bulb on the background of a shining sun with the amulet being encircled by small vines.

History
After the war against the Tuigan Horde ended in the Vast, a young priest of Chauntea took over the Morden Winery located in a valley outside the village of Dragon Falls. But soon after, the priest – Larn, was petrified by the maedar winemaker Vorlon and his consort – medusa Samanthiss. For the decade that followed, Larn was left a statue with the medallion of proof, adorned with Chauntea's holy symbol hanging on his neck. Circa the, the medallion of proof could've been given to a group of heroes from Ravens Bluff as a reward for killing the medusa or peacefully negotiating the vinery and Larn's freedom.

Merlot and its fellow stray mutt Wander found their way to the Morden Winery sometime before the . The digs were fed and taken care of by the winery's inhabitants – medusa Samanthiss and her maedar mate – Vorlon. Eventually, the strays became loyal guard dogs for the couple and could often be found patrolling the winery, along with other creatures that defended it. Their keen sense of smell allowed for early intruder detection, and loud barking alerted the maedar and the medusa.

Personality

One of his bracers could be used to ring a gong.

He watched the door for Lady Joysil Ambrur, under the supervision of the steward Elward Daunthideir.

History
Melarvyn refused entry to Roldro Tattershar in the ; he summoned the steward, Elward, when Roldro refused to leave. After Roldro threatened the steward with revealing his secrets, he was granted permission to enter; Melarvyn wondered if the steward would let him live after what he'd witnessed.

## Meleghost Telchaedrin

Meleghost Telchaedrin was a Halruaan nobleman, wizard and adventurer in the 14 century DR.

Description
He was very tall, with a very long nose. His breath smelled of old spices, which was somehow pleasant.

Abilities
He knew many spells, which included teleport and almost certainly fly. He possessed some means to mindscry people at a distance, and used the power to chat.

Possessions
His home had at least one high-vaulted hall, with a round window as tall as six men.

Activities
Meleghost used to adventure in his youth.

Relationships
One of the people Meleghost adventured with was Thabrant Swordsilver. He was an older uncle of Sarde Telchaedrin.

History
Caladnei's last mission with the Brightstar Sash was a request from Sarde Telchaedrin; during its completion, she received a missive from Lord Meleghost Telchaedrin. She went to meet him at his home, and he told her that their mission was a ruse by Sarde to get them to attack a rival family, instructing her to flee Halruaa immediately and alone. He also revealed he knew Caladnei's father, Thabrant Swordsilver, and that they were friends. The man explained that now that Thabrant Swordsilver was dying, his pride was gone and he just wanted Caladnei back with him for the end. To this end, he yielded unto her the teleport spell; Caladnei insisted on trading him a spell of flight. Though he claimed he already had it, he accepted hers.

Rumors & Legends
People in Halruaa considered Lord Meleghost Telchaedrin an "odd one".

## Melodia Thorm

Melodia Thorm was a half-elf of House Thorm, a follower of Selûne, and the wife of General Ketheric Thorm, as well as the mother of Isobel Thorm.

History

Prior to 986 DR, Melodia married Ketheric Thorm, and the marriage was a genuine love match. The union bore a daughter, named Isobel Thorm. Morfred, the individual whom Ketheric commissioned to build Moonrise Towers, believed that it was through Melodia's influence that Ketheric converted from his previous deity to Selûne, Melodia's patron. At some point, Melodia died of an illness, leaving a grief-stricken Ketheric to raise Isobel alone, though Isobel would soon die after her mother. In his grief, Ketheric abandoned Selûne and turned to Shar, beginning a plan to eliminate all Selûnite presence within Reithwin, with this eventually concluding by 1373 DR.

Despite this, however, Ketheric still thought highly of his late wife, and believed she would not recognize the man she loved if she were still alive. Isobel likewise shared his sentiment, and reflected she missed her greatly. Several busts, whether contemporary or not, with Melodia's likeness were found within Moonrise Towers as of 1492 DR, and one existed in Isobel's former room. Isobel also had a painting of her parents in her possession.

Circa the, Mister Mercer was in charge of the help that served Lady Firelia Jannath in her mansion. When her new husband fell victim of a ghostly possession, Mercer dismissed all servants from the mansion on the mistress's orders, leaving only her loyal butler Tarhun Mnemonis standing guard of the mansion's main entrance. Mercer placed Furbleston, Bailey, Cyrilla, Jooli, Phubbs, and Chef Marjoram on temporary leave with the pay of half-wages and allowance to seek temporary employment somewhere else. The chamberlain issued a statement that this dismissal was done at no imputation of malfeasance and would be ended upon notice.

## Merchant Square (Neverwinter)

The Merchant Square of Neverwinter was the financial hub of that city prior to the eruption of Mount Hotenow.

Geography
The Merchant Square was located in Neverwinter's Tower District.

History
In the years prior to the eruption of Mount Hotenow in the, the Merchant Square was a marketplace where merchants from all across the Sword Coast came to do business. The amount of money exchanged daily nearly rivaled the markets of Waterdeep.

After the volcanic eruption, the area was abandoned and came to be infested with elemental weevils. The vast wealth of the merchants was swiftly pillaged by bandits.

As of, the Merchant Square had come to be occupied by orcs from Many-Arrows, who clashed for control of the Tower District with the Neverwinter Guard. At the same time, a cell of Nasher rebels took up residence in the sewers, led by the half-orc Karr Nevrim.

Notable Locations
 The Merchant Guild Hall, a once-extravagant building where merchant leaders would meet in the years prior to the cataclysm.
 The Merchant Square sewers,  which were badly damaged and partly collapsed after the eruption, but served as a hideout for bandits and rebels into the late 15 century DR.

## Meerna

Meerna was a deep gnome Burrow Warden of Clan Ironhand, who lived in the city of Baldur's Gate during the late 15 century DR. She was part of an unfortunate excursion to the Underdark that led to much of her clan being killed or captured by the Cult of the Absolute.

Description
Meerna had gray skin and mane of white hair gathered in a bushy ponytail.

Activities
Meerna kept a detailed book of reports during the exodus to the Underdark. The book's last entry on the "Expedition #42" reported on the trip to the northern quadrant and a discovery of two potential mining sites and a vein of arandur. The book stated that the gnomes began mining under the supervision of Meerna's brother Welso, who she thought to eventually make a fine Burrow Warden.

History
In the, Meerna, her brother Welso, and the other deep gnomes of Clan Ironhand journeyed down to the Underdark from Baldur's Gate, on a mission to uncover the mythical substance known as runepowder. While searching for the long-lost site known as Grymforge, they were attacked by cultists of the Absolute led by Gekh Coal and other duergar slavers. Meerna was among those who survived and was condemned to working for the cultists at the Grymforge. It was likely Meerna had perished in a lake of lava, kicked into it by the True Soul Nere while within the Grymforge.

## Meteor

Meteors were small rocky or metallic objectsThe proper terminology would be "meteoroids", but this has rarely ever been used in a Realms source. The term "meteorites", which has been more commonly used over the years as a synonym, referred to meteoroids that had entered a planet's atmosphere. found in Wildspace. When meteors entered the atmosphere of a planet, or the air envelope of any larger body, the gravitational pull dragged them down. During this entry period meteors rapidly heated up and friction with the air caused them to ignite and catch on fire. This state was variously referred to as a meteor, shooting star, or falling star. A group of falling meteors was called a "meteor shower" and the event of one finally colliding with the ground was referred to as a "meteor strike". This was considered by many on Toril to be among the rarest and most destructive of cataclysmic events that could occur.

Properties
Meteors and meteorites ranged in size considerably, from microscopically small to incredibly large.

Meteors were known to contain a variety of different materials that they distributed to planets through their crashing. The most notable of these was star metal, which could be used to make various enchanted armor and weaponry, and meteorite iron. Other materials that could potentially be found in meteors included adamantine, agni mani, skydrop, and allegedly the metal hellthorn.

Some meteors, referred to as star-spores, were infested with strange fungi.

While meteors could potentially land anywhere on Toril, they had a propensity for landing within its deserts, such as the Anauroch and the Raurin Desert. This led to the creation and distribution of tektites—glass-like objects formed from the heat of meteors meeting sand — such as Agni mani and skydrop, across the sands of Anauroch and other Faerunian deserts. Additionally, the impact of meteors with deserts could transform the sand around their impact point into slipsand.

Usage
 Some kept meteorites purely as decorative display pieces, as was the case with Sibonseni, who kept a handful in her private villa.
 Making a ring of shooting stars could require pieces of meteorites.

Reputation
Some cultures in the Realms tied religious significance to meteors or meteor showers. In the Moonshae Isles, this natural phenomenon was one of many that was believed to be a sign that one of the Earthmother's children had passed away. Among the Elven pantheon, the deities Corellon and Sehanine Moonbow sometimes displayed their favor to worshipers by manifesting a meteor. Sehanine was also known to show her favor through meteor showers, while Corellon sometimes displayed his disfavor with someone by manifesting a shooting star that would look to those below as if it had fallen from between the tips of a crescent moon like some kind of heavenly tear.

Within the Maztican pantheon, meteors were associated with the god Kukul. Among the Mulhorandi pantheon, a shooting star was said to indicate the presence of Horus-Re at night.

Notable Meteors
 Sometime in Toril's distant past, a cataclysmic meteor strike in the Frozenfar left an unusual rift exposing rich veins of copper and silver, which later became the site of the mining town Fireshear.
 Sometime around Midsummer in -5000 DR, a meteor fell upon the central settlements of elven realm of Uvaeren. In the wake of that fallen star came a widespread meteor shower, whose stars all set aflame trees across western Arcorar for a period of twelve days. These fires cleared a wide gash into the forest along the direction of the first meteor, decimating the Lorelands of Uvaeren and separating Rystall Wood from the rest of the Cormanthor.
 The Hammer of Fire was a massive fiery meteor that was on a collision course with Toril sometime in the late 14 century DR. It had the potential to be the most cataclysmic event in the planet's history, but a group of Ravenian adventurers were entrusted with the task of stopping it by the city's Wizards Guild. Provided with the tinker gnome Ignious Rammousdammous as a spelljamming helmsman, they borrowed the space galleon Mooney's Marauder and took off into Realmspace with twenty powderkegs of blast powder, gifted to them by Iggy's associate, the giff Major Plunker Big-Boom. Though they were delayed by the efforts of Talosians, the adventurers managed to destroy the Hammer of Fire before it could reach the planet.
 A meteorite that fell into the Elven Woods on Toril thousands of years before the Time of Troubles was responsible for shaping the land into what would later be known as Mistledale.
 A meteor that fell to Toril in primeval times was widely believed to be the source of hellthorn. Many rumors surrounded the meteorite, with some speculating it housed a diabolic entity or a fragment of Asmodeus's original divinity. In early 1479 DR, the priests among the devil-worshiping duergar that took control of Gauntlgrym believed that the meteorite was somewhere beneath that ancient dwarven city.
 The Great Rift in the city of Omu was created by a meteorite that was carrying russet mold spores, leading to the creation of vegepygmies in the surrounding area.
 The eldritch Star Worm was brought to Toril in circa 400 DR by a meteorite twice the size of a temple that fell near Ixtzul Valley in Maztica's Far Payit region.
 The Tear of Selûne, a diamond approximately the size of an ogre's fist that fell to the cold lands of Thar on Toril during the Time of Troubles.
 A great meteorite containing star metal struck the island of Tuern in 1358 DR. Its starmetal quickly became of great interest to both the Kraken Society and local fire giants. The Heroes of Ascore managed to recover some of this starmetal, bringing to Allis Lhyssich's Hand Crafted Weapons and Magical Weapons for Noble Causes to be forged into the sword of stonecutting.

History
Over a hundred myths across Faerûn claimed that the Sea of Fallen Stars was created by a massive meteor that fell from the Tears of Selûne, or from even further beyond the moon Selûne. According to the Parwiccan Cycle, it was more of a shower of meteors that fell in, creating a series of natural catastrophes across Toril that destroyed whole continents in earthquakes, fires, and windstorms, rearranging even the positions of the four inner seas. Ancient sarrukh accounts called this event the "changing of the stars", while other people referred to it as the Tearfall.

The founder of the S'tung Monastery in T'u Lung, known posthumously by the title Sky Father, had a keen interest in astronomy and left behind numerous records of comets and shooting stars that flew across the skies of Kara-Tur.

The Starmetal Hills in the Sword Coast North were the subject of numerous meteor showers from the 13 to 14 centuries DR, eventually making themselves the namesake of that range of knolls.

In the 14 century DR, some sages speculated that the three large shallow basins on Falx were evidence of gigantic meteorites having struck the planet in the distant past. These same sages further speculated that such cataclysmic impacts could have altered the climate of Falx, causing the extinction of most life forms on the planet's surface.

In, a meteor crashed into the merchant's district of Uwaji on the island nation of Wa, killing two hundred people. A wu jen by the name of Shiz-ti was later apprehended under suspicion of having caused the fallen star.

Sometime in the mid-14 century DR, a meteor containing a rich deposit of platinum fell into the Dragon Reach and was claimed by a group of aquatic dwarves.

Around 1367 DR, on the night that Adil al-Muluki stole the Seal of Suhail min Zann from a rotund priest outside the Mosque of a Thousand Fountains in the city of Muluk, nine meteors streaked across the night sky.

Aroyund the, across the continent of Faerûn lands far and wide were beset by tremendous lightning strikes, some of which would mark the impact points of a year long rain of meteors. Each of these meteors held within them some form of dragon egg. Worshipers of Bahamut and Tiamat received visions from their respective draconic god regarding these meteor strikes, instructing them to seek out the fallen stars.

## Methild

Methild was the court bard of the ancient kingdom of Chilldion as well as the namesake, and possibly the creator, of Methild's harps, who lived long before the 14 century DR.

History
The legend that told Methild's story stated that he was a bard who lived in a tiny valley kingdom called Chilldion far from other civilized realms. One winter night, a coven of three hags took over the valley after ensorcelling the royal family. The hags were planning on eating the family whilst they were immobilized by the foul holding magics. After a long day of conquest, spellcasting, and slaughtering of the innocent, the coven grew tired and ordered Methild, the court bard, to strum them a song. But the bard was loyal to his king and brought along his enchanted harp. He played the instrument and focused its magics on the king first, dissolving the holding spell and allowing the king to sneak away. Methild continued to play and remove magic from everyone restrained by the hags, one by one. When every victim was freed, Methild could play no longer, and the coven realized the treachery. The bard did not escape and became the hags' supper instead of the king's family.

## Mempter's barrier

Mempter's barrier was a defensive evocation spell from the 6 circle of magic that was somewhat similar to the wall of force arcane magic spell, developed by the powerful elementalist Mempter.

Effects
Mempter's barrier conjured forth a magical force field, similar to the one created via wall of force, but, unlike it, Mempter's barrier allowed one-pay passage through. It allowed creature from the inside of the barrier to attack through with weapons, spells, or ranged arms, pass though, but not back, also blocking anything that was trying to penetrate it from the outside, including projectile spells, missiles, and creatures. The magical barrier was immovable and invisible, and could be erected at the distance of  from the caster, and its dimensions were a cube or a sphere of up to  squared, increasing with the caster's powers. The duration, like the size, increased with the caster's magical prowess growing. The caster could end the spell at will.

Mempter's barrier was unaffected by dispel magic and most other spells. It was immune to any physical, cold, heat, lightning, dragon's breath, and other types of damage. The barrier immediately disappeared is hit with the disintegrate, spell or via the use of a sphere of annihilation or a rod of cancellation.

Components
The spell required verbal, somatic, and material components to be cast. Its material component was powdered diamond worth of 5,000 gp, and a mirrored lens.

History
Mempter's barrier was the creation of the mysterious human, by some accords, or a Djen of Calimshan, by others. The wizard settled in the city of Ravens Bluff in the mid-to-late 14 century DR, already possessing the knowledge of the Mempter's barrier spell.

This power could make natural weapons such as claws and jaws, as well as those provided by powers such as claws of the beast or bite of the wolf, act as though they were weapon with a +1 or greater enchantment. ## Metafaculty

Metafaculty was a psionic power of the clairsentience discipline that allowed a manifester to receive a remarkably accurate vision of a creature and its whereabouts.

Effects
Those who employed metafaculty raised their minds to a near-universal level of consciousness in the process, allowing them to meditate over numerous impressions and predictions regarding a certain target. This target creature had to be someone the psionic user had seen prior, either in person or remotely through another power, such as remote viewing. The power took around an hour to manifest and had an average duration of one minute, extended by one or more minutes depending upon how skilled the psionic user was.

Once metafaculty was fully manifested, the user would receive various visions regarding the target's personal nature, location, and activities. For the remaining duration of the power, the user would also receive a mental view of the creature, as though they were using the remote reviewing power.

Details on the target's personal nature included such things as their name, health, race, class, alignment, and any significant items they had on their person.

Locational premonitions included the target's place of residence, what town or country they were currently in, to as far out as what world in the Prime Material plane they were currently on or what plane in the multiverse they were in.

Activities included anything significant that the target had done within the prior 8 hours – what places they had traveled through, the names or races of creatures they fought, what (if any) spells they had exist, and items they had acquired or left behind, as well as the location of said items.

What was considered "significant" by these premonitions could vary depending upon an individual manifester's own biases. For example, a powerful seer would likely overlook a routine meal or the presence of a lowly potion of cure light wounds on their target, instead finding attendance of a banquet or the presence of a potion of supreme healing to be significant.

Metafaculty could potentially overcome most psionic powers, spells, and abilities that normally obscured clairsentience powers. This even included such things as mind blank, screen, and wish. However, this power could not overcome epic spells or epic powers that obscured clairsentience powers and divinations.

Displays
When used this power manifested mental, olfactory, and visual displays.

Users
This power was knowable by seers, as well as ardents and divine minds that followed the psionic mantles of communication or fate.

Known/Notable Users
 Raulothim, an emerald great wyrm.

## Melverfew

Melverfew was a cheap stewed dish favored by the lower class Waterdhavians as of the late 15 century DR.

Description
Melverfew was made using diced eels, flounder, and harbor catfish, simmered until soft with big fleshy mushrooms in an oxen-and-onion gravy. The meat then was strained out, deboned, and returned into the stew with added shredded stale bread. Lastly, a handful of grapes or gooseberries as well as raisins were added in and cooked further.

Melverfew was a cheap staple, enjoyed by many, but celebrated by few. It was priced at a single copper piece per a large bowlful. That copper also bought a mug of small beer that came together with the stew.

## Mental Power mantle

The Mental Power mantle was a psionic mantle that granted ardents and divine minds an array of psionic powers and abilities derived from the cosmic philosophy of the power of the mind.

Deities

Abilities
Those who followed the Mental Power mantle gained a greater capacity for reserving psychic energy within their mind.

Divine minds of the Mental Power mantle exuded a psychic aura that made them and any allies within the radius of their aura more resistant to mind-affecting spells and abilities.

Powers
Novice manifesters could disrupt the minds of others with id insinuation and mental disruption, as well as assault the mind with mind thrust. Manifesters of some experience could protect the mind with intellect fortress and strike back at telepathic attacks with mind trap. As a manifester progressed further they learned how to crush mental essence with psychic crush and explode the heads of their enemies with cranial deluge. Masters of this mantle obtained hypercognition, allowing them to enhance the logic centers of their mind.

## Meira

Meira the Dark was a priestess of Shar near Neverwinter in the mid-to-late 14 century DR.

Description
She was simultaneously gaunt and "toadlike", yet wrinkled, but with glossy raven hair, and glittering green eyes.

Personality
With a penchant for grim humor and quick wit, Meira was nonetheless marked by the scorn she had for all good folk, her cannibalism, and her hatred of the word "why".

Abilities
She wielded magic that changed the looks of another both temporarily and for good, as well as spells that destroyed magic devices.

Possessions
The woman lived in a filthy cave near Neverwinter, but still had a few enchanted possessions at hand, including a magic-dead ring.

Relationships
She was part of a nameless cabal that worked intrigues all over Faerûn.

History
When Auvrarn Labraster sought succor after escaping Silverymoon by using a teleport ring, he sent himself to the abode of Meira the Dark. There, she aided him, working spells to destroy his magic items, preventing him being tracked through them, then burning his clothes, and finally reshaping him into the likeness of Azoun IV. She then spent the night with him, reshaping herself into some other wench, unlike her own deliberately disgusting shape.

In the morning, she reshaped him instead into the likeness of Blandras Nuin, a man she'd recently sacrificed in some dark rite, who owned a home in nearby Neverwinter, and gave him a magic-dead ring that thwarted just about all magic.

## Metamorphosis

Metamorphosis, formerly known as shape alteration, was a psionic science of the psychometabolism discipline that allowed a manifester to assume the form of a creature, object, plant, or rock of a comparable mass.Only The Complete Psionics Handbook and Player's Option: Skills & Powers bring up the possibility of transforming into a "rock" or "tree". The 1, 3, and v3.5e versions of metamorphosis do not explicitly state whether or not such transformations are possible. It was similar to the magical effect of polymorphing, though with wider applications.

Effects
All versions of metamorphosis shared the effect of not granting manifesters any special qualities possessed by the form they had assumed (such as tremorsense or regeneration), nor any unique abilities, including supernatural, psionic, and spell-like abilities. For example, if a manifester took the form of a red dragon they would not exhibit their fiery breath weapon.

A manifester would be granted the natural physical qualities of any form they assumed, such as proportional strength, mundane methods of locomotion (such as burrowing, flight, swimming), natural armor, and natural methods of attack (if any), including poison, whilst retaining their own mind. If an assumed creature form was capable of speech, the manifester could communicate as usual. And if a manifester took the form of an object, plant, or rock they would lose all mobility, but retain their normal senses and capacity to speak.

Any garments or equipment worn by the manifester would either be altered to conform to their new body shape,The 2 edition iteration of metamorphosis presented in The Complete Psionics Handbook and Player's Option: Skills & Powers makes no mention as to what occurs to a manifester's clothing and equipment when they assume a new form. or meld into the new form and reappear exactly as they were once the manifested reverted their form. If an assumed form was wearing or wielding anything that the manifester's base form could feasibly wear or carry in an equivalent body part, then it would still be held at the time of reversion.

Prior to the Time of Troubles,The loss and stabilization of psionics mentioned in Forgotten Realms Adventures and The Complete Psionics Handbook are in-universe references to the initial lack of psionics in 2 edition AD&D, and the reintroduction of psionics with a new ruleset, respectively. Though this in-universe explanation is somewhat contradicted by the use of psionics set in Toril's past, such as Netheril: Empire of Magic. shape alteration was quite similar to the spell polymorph self. During that time, any additional animal, mineral, or vegetable material that a manifester had on their person would be counted towards their overall weight for a transformation. Shape alteration also differed from later versions of the power in that the amount of psychic energy required for a manifestation was not static, but varied depending upon the overall weight of the form desired.

Following the Time of Troubles and fundamental changes in how psionics worked on Toril, metamorphosis was found to cause great physical stress upon a manifester. The shock to a manifester's system could be so bad that they passed out, but still expended psychic energy from the attempt. On some rare, lucky occasions a manifestation allowed for assuming the form of something three times the size of the manifester's body mass. And if a manifestation backfired, there was a chance the psionics user could become trapped in their new form.

By the late 14 century DR, the scope of metamorphosis had become far more narrowed. A manifester could not transform into a construct, elemental, outsider, undead, or template creature. Nor could a manifester assume a form that was smaller than, gaseous, ectoplasmic, or incorporeal. The capacity to transform into objects remained, though complex objects and mechanical mechanisms required some expertise associated with it. Additionally, metamorphosis did not allow for transforming into magic items, psionic items, psionically animated mechanisms, or objects formed out of ectoplasm. If destroyed or slain, the manifester's body would revert to its original form.

In whatever form a manifester took, they would any special attacks or qualities of their base form, minus those that required a particular limb. Additionally, a manifester had the freedom to designate physical qualities of their new creature form, so long as they were within the normal ranges of what was possible for that species. These included such things as hair color and texture, height, sex, skin color, and weight.

Displays
This power exhibited material and olfactory displays when manifested.

Users
This power was knowable by psionicists, particularly those who specialized as egoists, wild talents, and ardents and divine minds that followed the Natural World mantle.

Metamorphosis was exhibited as an innate psionic ability by amethyst dragons, baku, beljuril dragons, greater shedu, greater ssvaklors, and topaz dragons. Additionally, shades of those who possessed psionics in life often developed shape alteration.

In psionics terms, metamorphosis could be known by couatl.

## Metapsionic

The metapsionics discipline was a theoretical advanced discipline of psionics that primarily revolved around enhancing, augmenting, or amplifying other psionic powers or abilities. All of its powers fell under the purview of the other psionic disciplines, such as cannibalize, empower, and psychic surgery. Some referred to it as, rather than a discipline, its own unique subset of psionic abilities that augmented psionic powers, such as doubling their effective range, splitting a ray-attack into two, or making a power manifest twice. With the right skills in creating psionic items, one could store a metapsionic power within a dorje, power stone, or psionic tattoo.

Notable Users
 Thought eaters exhibited a unique metapsionic sense that detected psionic activity within the Border Ethereal and Prime Material. This power was always active and did not tax a thought eater's pool of mental energy.

Appendix
Background
Metapsionics debuted in 2-edition AD&D in the pages of The Complete Psionics Handbook (1991). It was later erased from that same edition in the pages of Player's Option: Skills & Powers (1995), which redistributed its powers to the other five disciplines. The term was repurposed in the 3 and 3.5 editions as one of three types of psionic feats, mirroring the metamagic feats of those editions.

## Mercykillers

The Mercykillers, also known as the Red Death, were one of the fifteen sanctioned philosophical and political factions of the Outer Planes. Originally based in Sigil, and later the stronghold called the Tower of Law in the Astral Sea. The core tenet of this faction was that the entire multiverse would become better through justice, as justice would purge an individual's evil and make them better. Once everyone was cleansed, the multiverse would reach perfection.

Activities
The faction once ran the prison in Sigil, acting as the wardens and every so often hunting down those already condemned but not yet imprisoned. They did not make laws nor pass judgement, only administered punishment without leniency.

During the tenure of Factol Alisohn Nilesia, a select few Mercykillers chosen personally by the Factol herself became Justiciars and would hunt down those already convicted but yet to be imprisoned through the darkest pits and farthest points of the multiverse. They devoted their lives to chasing down heinous criminals that had slipped through the fingers of the law.

Base of Operations
The Mercykillers were originally based in the Sigil Prison from where they hunted down criminals of all types and brought them to justice. After the Faction War in Sigil, the remaining Mercykillers re-established themselves on a floating rock in the Astral Sea and built the Tower of Law, a grim fortress of fifteen levels, that held prisons, torture chambers, barracks, treasuries, and more. From this fastness they would mount forays into the other planes by way of a permanent teleportation circle inscribed in the Vanguard Room. They would round up prisoners accused of whatever crimes they decide upon during the raid and drag them back to their fortress to face the Red Death's brand of justice.

Possessions
Mercykillers wore bladed armor and at least one pair of manacles. Often a mask or hood would obscure their faces, ensuring the face of justice was detached from its duty.

Relationships
The faction was allied with the Harmonium and the Fraternity of Order, as the Harmonium patrolled and caught the criminals, the Fraternity of Order sentenced the criminals for their crimes, and the Mercykillers jailed the criminals and kept them imprisoned until their sentences were over. The Mercykillers saw the Sign of One, Society of Sensation, and Revolutionary League as enemies and often clashed with members of those factions, especially those of the Revolutionary League as they sought to destroy the other established factions, particularly those that held up the system of Sigil.

History
The Mercykillers came to be during the Great Upheaval as the Sodkillers and Sons of Mercy united in their pursuit of justice and created their Eight Tenets of Justice more commonly known as the Mercykiller Code. For 633 years, they were the wardens and jailers of Sigil until the Faction War saw all factions disbanded or exiled from Sigil. The Mercykillers tore themselves back into the two factions of Sodkillers and Sons of Mercy, with the Sons of Mercy keeping the job as wardens of the Sigil prison. Many of those Mercykillers that were not in Sigil during this time however joined neither, and instead became roaming groups. That was until a Mercykiller named Andrezhej established a new fortress in the Astral Sea, and built the Tower of Law. From here the Mercykillers acted more like their Justiciars of old, hunting down and dragging back those who they thought guilty of crimes before either incarcerating them at the tower or executing them for their crimes.

Members
Members joined simply by showing up on the enlistment days that were held once every fortnight. If the potential member had no outstanding issues with the law and was willing to listen to the Eight Tenets of Justice and swear upon each one, then they were henceforth considered a Mercykiller.

## Megalomania

Megalomania was a delusional state of mind recognizable as a form of madness, wherein the person believed themselves to be far greater than they truly were. It had similarities with delusional insanity, which was even less connected to reality.

Effects
A megalomaniacal individual believed that they were truly exceptional in some way—for example, that they were the smartest, strongest, most famous, or most beautiful person in the world, or most capable and notable in some other way. They naturally thought themselves fit for leadership and chafed under the commands of lessers (everyone), and took extreme offense to any suggestion that their delusions were not entirely true. They could very easily get themselves into trouble thanks to their inability to accurately judge their own merits, and if given authority over others they had the potential to cause even larger problems.

Notable Afflicted
Creatures
 Most beholders believed that they were the supreme and most powerful example of their kind, and that all others were inferior or degenerate. This was matched by extreme paranoia towards imagined threats and xenophobia regarding all other beings.
 Dragons were quite vain and proud compared to other creatures, but overall remained quite stable and sane within those norms. Red dragons were the most likely to fall to full-blown delusional megalomania. Gold dragons were overall the most mentally stable of dragonkind, but megalomania was the most likely condition if they did suffer anything.

Individuals
 Balagos was exceptionally megalomaniacal even for a red dragon. He believed that he was the destined ruler of not just all dragons, but the whole world as well.
 Edwin Odesseiron, a Red Wizard of Thay, consistently overestimated his ability and underestimated that of others. While indeed a powerful mage, he was immediately deposed after briefly conquering Thay, and was handily defeated and humiliated by the "inferior" Elminster.
 Lord Hyraken of Hilp was fantastically self-centered and believed himself the rightful king of Cormyr.
 Halsaime of Druid's Deep went insane with power and grandeur after being corrupted by a Shadovar staff.
 Vulkaran the Dark was deluded by his own accomplishments, and ultimately replaced his people's own gods.
 Zarr was a warrior who was struck by magic and afflicted with megalomania. He believed that he was a leader of supreme ability and destined to go down in history as the greatest of generals.

## Meatbread

Meatbread was a Sembian dish consisting of mincemeat and the salted, pre-boiled leftovers of slaughterhouses, all baked together into a loaf of wheat bread. Its taste profile was that of savory and mildly spiced flavor. Its mincemeat fillings typically included leeks, old sharp cheese, and the herb dill.

Availability
This was an affordable handfood in the city of Selgaunt in the 14 century DR, considered a staple among those who lived in the Foreign District.

History
In the,The novel is undated, but in Chapter 1 it has been "nearly two years" since events in Shadow's Witness, which is dated to Hammer, 1371 DR. Since Hammer is the first month in the Calendar of Harptos, this implies late 1372 DR or Hammer 1373 DR. Apparently some time later, in Chapters 2 to 4, it is spring and in Chapter 9, it is the "seventeenth day of the sixth month", Kythorn 17. Therefore, Kythorn, 1373 DR is presumed. when visiting the city of Selgaunt after a long absence, a hungry Erevis Cale was provoked by his growling stomach to stop by a small bakery on his way to the Gilt Lizard. There he paid a baker two ravens for a day-old loaf of meatbread. He began snacking on the meatbread as he sat in the public plaza, waiting for a bell to pass. Cale remarked to himself that the dish's taste was just as inadequate as he had remembered it, since the last time he had ate it years prior, yet by the time he was finished with it he wished that he had bought two loaves.

## Mei Ling

Princess Mei Ling was the youngest daughter of Emperor Kai Tsao Shou Chin of Shou Lung.

Description
Mei Ling was noted to have inherited her mother's striking beauty, much like her older half-sister.

Personality
Mei Ling, like her older half-sister, was an intelligent and accomplished woman.

Relationships
Mei Ling secretly dated Gisen, a prince of T'u Lung, for several months in . However, that relationship was cut short after her father deduced from a fictionalized song about their romance written by the traveling minstrel Tan Hikao Nong that they had been in love.The tale of Mei Ling's romance with a T'u Lung prince is retold in both Hall of Heroes and The Eastern Realms; however, while the former names Gisen as her lover, the latter names Wai Gada Anku as the prince in question. The Eastern Realms, which is set in 1357 DR, makes no statement about Anku's age but seems to imply he is a young adult; however, later sources place his birth year a mere five years before The Eastern Realms, thus rendering him incompatible with Mei Ling. This article assumes that the account told in Hall of Heroes is the correct version and that Tan Hikao Nong, the narrator of the T'u Lung chapter, had mistaken one prince for another or obtained his information from a source that did.

History
In, Mei Ling was captured by pirates, having likely been caught in the crossfires of their enmity with the Jade Throne, and subsequently rescued by a certain young merchant captain (in actuality, the disguised Prince Gisen). Following this chance encounter, the two of them became close companions and eventually lovers, but it was only after they had already fallen deeply in love that the two discovered each others' secret identities. Nonetheless, though the lovers knew their romance was doomed by the rivalry between their nations, they continued to meet in secret whenever possible under the auspices of Kwan Ying.

Several months later, Tan Hikao Nong, who was visiting the Shou Court at Kuo Te' Lung at the time, performed a ballad based on a fictionalized version of Mei Ling and Gisen's romance, singing of a kidnapped princess and her rescue by and subsequent forbidden romance with a brave prince. Most of the Court never caught on, simply enjoying the tale for the fictional love story it supposedly was, but the Emperor was able to connect the dots, linking it to the incident of Mei Ling's prior kidnapping and rescue. Mei Ling was subsequently taken away to an undisclosed location as her eldest brother sent assassins against Gisen, but Gisen never gave up on her, biding his time as he traveled to Kozakura and Wa to find allies and magics that might aid in his quest.In Hall of Heroes, an otherwise-unknown character by the name of Tam Nong Hikong fills the role played by Tan Nong in The Eastern Realms. Given the name similarity and identical role, it is extremely likely that Tam Nong Hikong is a typo for Tan Hikao Nong; this is the assumption this article makes.
